The ancient Greek philosophers (beginning with Plato) supposed that the whole does not reduce to the simple sum of its parts. But all the most important suppositions and conjectures in this connection to this very day abide in the category of the theoretical (not to say scholastic) and have neither the status of recognized knowledge nor practical application. The official encyclopedic editions likewise limit themselves to very meager and vague utterances on this score [45, p. 768; 37, p. 1500]. The whole — this is not a simple sum (or totality) of its parts, but plus yet something else, obtained as a result of the interaction of the parts among themselves and of the parts with the whole. When the whole grows by parts in such a correlation with the whole as is prescribed by the

algorithm of the golden division, then the forming whole (under which are implied both the newly arising and the changing forms-relations) acquires the maximal contentful filling. Thus, the number "φ" is a quite concrete quantitative correlation between the whole and the part in certain relations-forms, answering to the maximally possible contentful filling of these forms-relations in the course of their growth by parts in the mentioned quantitative correlations with the whole at certain definite stages-levels of the development of these forms-relations. Precisely this correlation (this proportion) of the whole and the part answers to the highest contentfulness of the whole growing by a part in the indicated correlation. The regularity of the construction of the golden proportion described by Kepler in an astonishing way resembles the sequence of Fibonacci numbers. Astonishing, however, only at first glance. The abstract and, it would seem, unconnected with practical application numerical sequence of Fibonacci is nothing other than a model of the optimal, most contentful growth of the whole by parts, i.e., a model of the modification of existing and the formation of new forms of heightened (in the striving toward the highest) contentfulness. [A passing remark. Mathematics is excessively full of surprises, sometimes considered products of the fruitless play of a brilliant mind, but having, however, a direct relation to the objective reality, although not always an obvious one. The whole matter is in the excess, speaking simplistically, of the intensity of the content-form transformations at the level of human thinking over the analogous intensity at the level of the subject-practical activity of man, in consequence of which many contentful developments of the "pure" reason gather dust on the shelves, humbly awaiting their "binding" and demand at the level of formal practical embodiment.] It should be especially noted that under the term "golden section" and its numerical value there hides a boundary correlation between the components of the whole and the part with its (the correlation's) limit, guessed in the perspective of the infinite growth of the totality of the constituent parts in the whole, modeled (the speech is about the growth) by the ascending sequence of Fibonacci numbers. The analysis of the tendency of the change of the values of the relations of the neighboring members of the Fibonacci sequence, lying in the basis of the search for the most contentful correlation between the whole and the totality of its constituent parts in concrete forms-relations built after the type of the golden section, testifies to the fact that the increase of the number of parts included in the whole leads to the refinement of such a correlation and to its ever greater optimization, but the obtaining of an exhaustively exact numerical value answering to the most optimal correlation, even with an arbitrarily great (unlimited) growth of the quantity of parts in the composition of the whole (as also with the proportional increase of the

geometric dimensions of the whole and the parts), is impossible neither practically nor theoretically. In other words, the contentfully fulfilled whole is uncountable (does not yield to counting) and inexpressible wholly and without remainder by any number of limited parts and their totality; with the unbounded increase of the number of parts entering into the whole, modeled by the ascending Fibonacci sequence, the relation of the neighboring members of the sequence arbitrarily closely approaches a certain limit, but never attains it. Only in such an unfinished way and in an approximate manner is expressed (and can be expressed) the contentful fullness of the uncountable whole through the infinite succession of countable totalities of its parts. Thereby the analysis in the indicated connection of the unending sequence of Fibonacci numbers allows coming to an important conclusion about the principled unattainability of the exhaustive expression of the content of the whole by any formal totality of its parts, even with the unlimited increase of the quantity of parts in the totality. Continuing the investigation of this model (i.e., analyzing the variability of the values of the relations of the neighboring numbers of the Fibonacci sequence), one can discern also the presence here of a certain likeness of forms developing at different levels with different volumes of contentfulness (with the variation of the number of parts or of their sizes in the composition of these different-level forms), but the repetition (i.e., the equality or identity) of forms belonging to different levels with different volumes of contentfulness is unrealizable and impossible in principle! About this again testifies the infinite incompleteness and unattainability (in computation) of the exhaustively exact correlation designated by the irrational number "φ", observed (the speech is about the incompleteness and unattainability) in the investigation of the character of the change of the values of the relations of the neighboring members of the increasing Fibonacci sequence. The golden proportion, or the special correlation of the whole and the part designated by the number "φ", is an indicator of the heightened contentfulness of forms-relations of a definite type, manifesting itself through the optimality of properties-characteristics and through the aesthetic perfection of these forms. The irrationality of this number testifies both to the unattainability of the exhaustive expression of the highest contentfulness by the forms of such proportions, and to the possibility of the unlimited approximation to its exhaustive expression. The coming of the event of the exhaustive expressedness of the highest contentfulness by the form of the golden relation would mean that, beginning with some step of the realization of the algorithm of the computation of the constant "φ", the correlation of the whole and the part (of the golden proportion) would remain unchanged, i.e., limited by some quantity of signs (by any their quantity, but necessarily limited), and therefore unchanged. However, the infinity and non-periodicity of the fractional part (i.e., the irrationality) of the number displaying this correlation (as in the case with the number "π") speaks of the opposite.

As in the case with the number "π", the infinity and non-periodicity of the fractional part (the irrationality) of the number "φ" means that very barrier which separates two sides-constituents of one and the same relation {content/form} that are incompatible in exactness and non-interchangeable. As in the case with the same "π", again we note the infinite "run-up" or the unending transition from one component of reality to the other (from one region of reality — into the other) in their relation. If the case with the number "π" speaks of the impossibility of the whole expression of the boundless continuity and curvature by the limited discreteness and rectilinearity, then the case with the number "φ" testifies to the principled inexpressibility in an exhaustive manner of the contentful whole (again uncountable and continuous) by means of the totality of formally limited parts (i.e., by means of the countable and discrete). Despite the inseparability and interconnectedness of the contentful and formal sidesconstituents in the known relations "π" and "φ", the barrier between the contentful and formal components (in both relations) is essential to such a degree that it cannot be overcome by any successive or gradually-measured steps-approximations, however long these steps-approximations might be carried out. Even human thinking with its enormous potential is not capable of overcoming this barrier by measured gradualness; it is overcome exclusively by a leap. However, the latter always conceals in itself something not fully understood and not fully said. Partly for this very reason the nature and meaning of the fundamental world constants up to now nobody can properly explain. Thus, the number "φ" is such a special quantitative correlation between the components in the relation-form {whole/part} which answers to the maximally possible contentful filling (or the highest contentfulness) of this form-relation. At that, the irrationality of the number "φ" testifies to the principled impossibility of the establishment in exhaustive form of the final numerical correlation between the components of the relation-form {whole/part}, corresponding to its maximally contentful filling, and thereby to the impossibility of the acquisition of the highest contentfulness by this formrelation. The impossibility of the exhaustive expression of the highest contentfulness by the indicated form-relation is ascertained in the course of the modeling of the process of the acquisition (achievement) of this highest contentfulness by the procedure of the computation of the correlations of the neighboring members of the infinitely-increasing Fibonacci sequence. Undoubtedly, the number "φ", fixing a definite correlation of the components (sides-constituents) in certain concrete forms-relations, is connected with a certain optimality of these concrete forms-relations, conditioned by such a remarkable-kind correlation of components realized in them. At that, one

cannot but see that the internal contentful fullness of these forms is primary, while the optimality and the remaining external qualities-characteristics of such forms are derivative and secondary; in practice, however, everything is just the opposite — the formal side is taken for the initial one, and only then through it do they attempt to express the rationality and irrationality of all internal connections and relations. The heightened internal contentfulness of forms-relations, marked by the golden correlation of their components, sets the most optimal intensity of the development of such forms, ensuring their prolonged and productive contentful existence at certain stages. However, a similar scenario can be true and realizable only at definite segments of the development of these or those forms; with the change of conditions and circumstances (i.e., in the course of the inevitable further universal development) these or those bearers of golden proportions may lose their miraculous potential. For this reason it is very imprudent to be carried away by the excessive absolutization of the special correlations of components and of the formsrelations corresponding to them, predicting for such forms the main roles for all times. Real forms are different and all sorts, and therefore the entire spectrum of developing forms-relations is by no means exhausted by one (this or that) concrete correlation of their components. At the given stage of development precisely such a correlation of the constituent sides within certain forms-relations is and looks most optimal, stable, and expedient; at another stage under other conditions and circumstances this correlation may be forcedly transformed (just as over the centuries change the sympathies toward the proportions and beauty, say, of the female figure). Therefore to attempt to reduce all forms-relations to the golden relation, i.e., to absolutize, fetishize, mystify this relation and to spread the known myth (about the through presence of this correlation) wholesale over the entire host of unique developing forms — is absurd. Moreover, in the indicated connection the main task of man and of his thinking is to learn to foresee and anticipate the appearance of new correlations (including optimal ones, i.e., distinguished by heightened contentfulness) of the contentful and formal sides-constituents in their relations-forms at new stages of development, which, in fact, will determine the further progress of mankind. In general, all manner of assertions about the "final" discovery of the most final and perfect of forms (be it the golden section, or some "universal" mathematical formula, or some "super" technologies) contradict the very spirit of development, which, despite any "absolutes," breaks open and overcomes by its contentful side any form attempting once and for all to fix in itself and by itself any contentful movement-development; from definiteness => through indefiniteness => to new definiteness, — such is the most general form of the chain of the accomplished development. At that, the elements of indefiniteness and unpredictability of the course of development were, are, and will always be — in accordance with the formulation of the principle of self-development (the return to oneself, but every time to oneself changed, enriched, and developed). This context

presupposes as natural the representation of the fact that the moment of indefiniteness is also an objective and inalienable element of every development. Examples of the description of the optimal kind of all sorts of forms and processes, the construction, formation, or development of which submits to the regularities of the golden proportion, can be found in such printed works: [2, 14, 15, 19, 25, 40, 50]. § 3. With the examination of the essence of the number "e" the matter stands still more complicated In the scientific and educational literature the constant e is identified as the base of natural logarithms, which by no means introduces any additional clarity in our case. The constant e is not connected in an obvious way with simple geometric relations like the constants π (pi) and φ (phi) examined above. If the number π serves for the most part for the description of quite static forms-relations, the number φ — for the description of transitional forms ready for dynamic development, then the number e is present in the description of the most dynamic, in the fullest sense of the word, developing forms-relations-processes-systems, the components of which prove to be, first of all, such sides-constituents of reality as: {motion/rest}, {time/space}, {energy/mass}, {kinetic energy/potential energy}, {temperature/pressure}, etc. As the authors of modern publications consider: "If π is responsible for space, then e — for time and likewise manifests itself almost everywhere" [20]. In other words, e is responsible not for statics, but for dynamics, for the process, for changing and progressing forms (and not for forms for the most part object-like and static). Proceeding from this, one should expect that the interconnection and correlation of the components in their relation, designated by the number e, are more complicated and more contentful than in the relations of π and φ, since with respect to the constant e the speech goes about components of another "floor" of reality, of another level of contentfulness, than those levels which are occupied by the components of the relations of π and φ. Correspondingly to this, the numerical value of e fixes another, than π and φ, "boundary" and transitional correlation between the mentioned components in their relations. Besides, from the position of our representations about the development of reality (i.e., from the position of the concept of development) the aboveenumerated set of relations ({motion/rest}, {time/space}, {energy/mass}, {kinetic energy/potential energy}) enters into the "clip" or "sphere of influence" of such a generalized relation as {change/basis}. On this foundation, further investigations of the essence of the constant e we shall

conduct in the context of precisely this, most informative for us, initial relation. The relation {change/basis} — this is a completely different "class," a different level of forms-relations, than the relations {curvature/rectilinearity} and {whole/part} examined by us earlier (in the consideration of the constants π and φ). The form-relation {change/basis} differs from the two latter forms-relations by a more expressed dynamism, mobility, liveliness, changeability. But all this, as before, clarifies little; for a magic carpet by description may be both square and Persian, and take part in air parades, but the main thing (in the channel of our mood for the revelation of the inconspicuous essence) would still be the elucidation of that — what makes it a carpet-plane. Therefore, again, as in the cases with the numbers π and φ, let us turn to the most capacious definitions and interpretations of the constant e, as well as to the history of its appearance and origin. The initial and most effective, in our opinion, push or key to the comprehension of the sense of the number e is provided by its classical definition [8, p. 231; 26, p. 354]. Scrupulously examining this definition of the number e from the position of the concept of development mentioned already more than once, one can discover that under the very definition of the given number there hides a peculiar algorithm of the realization of the most effective way of the accumulation and assimilation of the accreting content by some form developing in the direction of the acquisition of the highest (maximally attainable) contentfulness! And again the speech goes about a certain contentfulness, which likewise served for us as a key moment in the course of the revelation of the essence of the constants π and φ. The form realizing itself by such an algorithm represents an optimal formation thanks to the way, following from the given definition, of the appropriation of the maximally possible volume of content accreting on the basis of a very limited initial contentful volume of the form itself and over a quite definite finite interval of time. On what is this assertion based? On the comparison of the textbook definition of the constant e with the way of the calculation of the compound bank interest accrued on a certain sum of the initial money deposit in the course of a definite time interval [31]. Strictly speaking, the history of the origin of the number e, as is noted in many literary sources, is connected precisely with the seeking-out of the maximal limit of the growth of the money deposit accreting with respect to its initial sum under the conditions of infinitely shortening intervals of time between the moments of the accrual of interest on the successively accreting at those same moments sum of this deposit. Only, in contrast to the history with the origin of the number e, in the case of the investigation of the definition of this number from the position of the mentioned concept of development under the sum of the deposit and the

bank interest there are implied a certain form of the initial content (basis) and a certain abstract content "winding" onto the basis (change), accreting to the basis by the algorithm corresponding to the above-mentioned way of the accrual of the bank interest. According to the algorithm used for the calculation of e, providing for ever more frequent (accelerating) interest accruals on the correspondingly accreting sum of the deposit, with such an increasing frequency accretes also the very sum of the deposit; a similar algorithm is realized, as we have already established, also in the computation of the ever more exact value of the constant φ by means of the infinite sequence of Fibonacci numbers. Only in contrast to the forms, the static proportions of which are determined by the measures of the number φ, the forms formed after the patterns of the number e are built by the dynamic path, with the participation of the stepby-step changing component of time, by virtue of which also in this latter case the accumulating content does not succeed in being expressed by a form wholly, — the state of contentful "saturation" is not attained (does not set in) in principle, since there is always, there is always present a "beyondform" leakage of content, despite the process of the successive growth, perfection, and raising of the degree of the "all-embracingness" of the form of the representation of this content. Hence, even in the regime of the rapidly developing dynamic layering of content (change), predetermined (the speech is about the regime) by the indicated algorithm of the computation of the number e, and even with the limited content of the isolated initial form (basis), it is impossible in the limit (fully and without remainder) to cover, absorb, and appropriate exhaustively all the accreting content by any final form whatsoever! Here we again (as in the case with the number φ) encounter the principally unattainable limit of the accumulation and expression by any perfect form whatsoever of exhaustively all the unswervingly accreting (in the course of the development of this form) content. It must be said that mathematicians, describing e as a remarkable limit of a certain functional relation, do not connect the algorithm or procedure of the computation of the value of this relation with any of the physical processes [2]; in other words, for the mathematical cast of mind the examination and solution of the task in the most general abstract formulation is a natural and habitual matter. In the case examined by us of the use of the algorithm of the computation of the number e for the calculation of contentful accumulations (changes) proceeding from the initial limited contentful volume (basis), the "denominator" (i.e., the basis) of the relation {change/basis} adduced earlier, like the "numerator" (change), directly participates in the contentful accretions and accumulations: changes do not occur on an empty place, but always "cling" to the basis (to the initial deposit, to some starting capital or contribution), and this concerns not only the development of specific forms-

relations in the domain of economics and finance, but also the development of any forms whatsoever and in any spheres whatsoever. A similar point of view on the correlation (interconnection) of the indicated components in the mentioned relation is considered self-evident (can one really separate the change itself in its "pure" form from that which changes?), — see, for example, about the connection of change and the stability of the properties, structure, and laws of the existence of bodies [44, p. 161]. Since the numerator of the relation ("change") is not essential by itself (outside the "basis"), then under the "change" of the numerator one should understand the "contentfully changing basis" (or the "basis accreting with content"). Under the "basis" of the denominator there is understood the "basis of the initial content." So, the analysis of the given algorithm shows that the maximal sum of the increment of the content of the changing basis, i.e., of the "numerator" of the relation, in comparison with the content of the initial basis, i.e., with the "denominator" of the relation, under the conditions of the realization of such a special algorithm (specific way of the increment of content) cannot go out beyond the limits of the excess of the contentful primary basis in e (as a certain definite number) times. Let us now examine, in what the specific algorithm of the calculation of the compound bank interest (i.e., of contentful accretions and accumulations in the context of our concept of development), coinciding in form with the algorithm of the computation of the constant e, differs from the other algorithms of bank accruals. According to the mechanism of the realization of the given algorithm, the maximal increment by content of a certain form of limited initial contentful volume in the course of a finite interval of time is attained under the condition that every infinitely small part of the contentful volume of the given form ensures an increment, externally accreting to this part of the contentful volume, proportional to the sum of the initial contentful volume of this part and of the contentful volume accreting to it in the course of periodically repeated contentful additions throughout the mentioned interval of time. By this somewhat ornate phrase here there is merely verbally transmitted the essence of the well-known mathematical expression for the computation of the constant e [8, p. 231; 26, p. 354], — and nothing more! In other words, thanks to such a way of accumulation (contentful enrichment), every particle, every smallest (infinitely small) element (at first of the initial, and then of the "layer-bylayer" accreting form) participates in full measure in the general single process of the accumulation and assimilation (assimilation) of the externally arriving content. And even under such a condition, the total volume of the content of the initial form (i.e., of the basis) and of the content externally arriving or accreting to the content of the initial form (i.e., of the change) is not capable of exceeding the contentful volume of the initial form (basis) more than in e times.

Thus, the number e is located on the boundary of the demarcation of the two components in the relation {change/basis} and binds them together by a definite dependence, conditioning the change (accumulation) of the content accreting to the basis (i.e., "sticking" or "winding" onto the initial, deliberately limited volume of the basis) in the total calculation, not exceeding the volume of the primary basis more than in e times. As it can now be seen, the algorithm of the calculation of e resembles the formulation of the principle of self-development. And in the one, and in the other cases there is present the dynamics of the development of the form in time and space. The difference consists only in the fact that the first variant describes the particular case of the development of a form of initially limited contentful volume (initial capital, a definite sum of a money deposit in a bank, some process in an isolated spatial volume, etc.) throughout a concrete finite time interval; the second, generalized variant is applicable to the description of the development of any forms (including open developing systems, as well as the totalities of systems as a whole) without any spacetime limitations. The conditions of the realization of such a special algorithm (answering to the above-noted particular case) presuppose the presence of explicit limitations both of the contentful and of the formal plan; there is evident the limitedness both in time of the accrual of the interest, and in the volume of the initial deposit (i.e., limitations of the space-time character). But along with this, all the advantages of such an isolated process are likewise demonstrated in full force. Being a particular case of the general principle of self-development, the algorithm of the calculation of the number e (similar in form to the process of the "winding" of content described by the formulation of the principle of selfdevelopment) allows separate forms under the specific conditions of the activity of their limited contentful primary basis in the course of finite time to accumulate the maximally possible content by virtue of the circumstance that every small particle, every smallest element of these separate forms participates in a direct and full-valued way in the process of contentful accumulation. Thus, the number e, or more precisely the algorithm of its obtaining (computation, calculation), represents a description of the most optimal way (procedure) of the assimilation and accumulation by some concrete separate (isolated) form of the maximal volume of the content arriving from outside. If in the cross-section of what has been said one imagines that the developing reality (i.e., the entire world surrounding us) as the universal form of a single complex process (not having limitations neither of the formal, nor of the contentful character, i.e., not limited neither in space, nor in time) were composed ("woven") of such maximally effective (optimal) small "e-forms," then this single universal big complex form would be

characterized by the highest contentfulness. However, the "harsh" reality introduces its corrections in the form of the interference and interpenetration of forms into the "fates" of one another. The "atypicalness" of such "e-forms" is conditioned by the circumstance that the given form is built on the basis of an initial form, contentfully limited and quite isolated from the interaction with other forms; a similar algorithm of development is not viable in application to the incomparably more widespread open forms-processes, which, by virtue of the forced interaction with other forms, develop by more general and less optimal patterns. Meanwhile, the "e-form" — this is a vivid example of the most optimal and effective development of a form within the framework of content-form limitations (i.e., limitations, in this case, of the space-time character). Because of the described limitations, a similar specific algorithm (after the type of the calculation of the number e) is not realizable in full measure with respect to the open forms-systems, having universal distribution and developing by the general patterns of the generalized formulation of the universal principle of self-development. The capitalization of a limited bank deposit or starting capital — this is almost the only specimens of really existing more or less closed isolated systems, to the description of the development of which the algorithm of the computation of the number e is ideally applicable: probably, for this very reason all literary sources, as if on command, refer exclusively to the example of the calculation (sanctified by the name of Bernoulli) of the compound usurious interest, — as to the example of a really functioning isolated system, guaranteeing the maximally attainable contentful accumulations under the conditions of known limitations. However, this by no means signifies that the advantages of such special forms are in any noticeable degree not realized in practice; the fact that the specific "e-forms" by urgent necessity are widely used by man in the form of dimensionless values in the description of a huge number of all sorts of real processes signifies that the real forms are oriented toward development after the image and likeness of the most contentful "e-forms," but by virtue of the merciless external influence, interference, and interpenetration on the part of the same forms the trajectory of their development can be substantially distorted and significantly differ from the most optimal one. In other words, the designation of the number e (preserving in the "folded" form the algorithm of the obtaining of this number), being present in various combinations with other parameters, variables, and arguments in formulaic expressions describing all sorts of real processes, indicates thereby although far from the highest, but still some optimality (heightened contentfulness) of the forms of such processes. Besides, the need for "e-forms" in the description of complex forms-processes is conditioned also by the circumstance that by these small forms there are "patched" the ruptures or steps on the boundaries of the division between the sides-constituents of reality, objectively taking place under the conditions of the development of big forms and processes; compromise small forms in similar cases are simply

necessary for the adequate and correct description of such really developing big processes and forms. Every form-relation-process has as the direction of its development the optimization of itself (which, strictly speaking, constitutes also the essence of the formulation of the principle of self-development). By this same circumstance there is conditioned also the presence-presence of optimizing values ("e-forms") in the formulaic expressions-descriptions of diverse real complex forms-relations-processes. As it follows from our investigation, the most effectively the task of the above-mentioned optimization is solved nevertheless by the "e-form," which, not having by virtue of the limitations accompanying it any noticeable distribution in the "pure" form, enters, however, as a component part and is extremely in demand in the description of optimally developing real general forms and processes. Along with this, the limitedness by the number-limit e — not the only remarkable feature of the scenario of the development of forms in the channel of the algorithm investigated by us. The second remarkable peculiarity of the given scenario — the principled unattainability of the limit of the highest (maximal) contentfulness and the inexpressibility of this limit in an exhaustively exact digital form. The process proceeding by such an algorithm has neither a platform, nor a point of "saturation." And again we encounter the relation of two components, whose transition from one to the other within the framework of the relation is impossible by a smooth measured course. In the role of the interacting and opposing components, as has already been noted, in this case there act the sides-constituents "change" and "basis." The relation composed of these components of reality (analogously to other, more concrete relations of the given type: {motion/rest}, {time/space}, {energy/mass}, {temperature/pressure}, etc.) is doomed to the presence (between the sides-components of this relation) of a barrier in the image of an irrational number, answering to a certain objective, although not expressible exhaustively, limit correlation of these components. And, finally, the third remarkable feature of the same scenario consists in the possibility of its infinite development and approximation to the expression of the highest contentfulness of the mentioned relation arbitrarily closely. Neither practically, nor theoretically is it possible to establish exhaustively exactly the correlation, and consequently, also the boundary of the division between the accreting contentful volume ("change") and the initial contentful volume ("basis"); in an analogous way it is impossible to determine also the exact boundary of the division between the components in the "kindred" relations from the same "clip": {motion/rest}, {time/space}, {energy/mass}... With whatever frequency and however long one may attempt to realize similar steps of approximations in the course of the realization of the algorithm analyzed by us, there will always remain some contentful part, not expressed, not calculated, and not subject to calculation by any rational form. And this barrier between the expressed content and the

content incapable of being expressed in all its fullness by any form whatsoever — is insurmountable in principle! In this very thing consists the sense of the irrational number e. The infinite non-periodic fraction of this number personifies that same infinite "run-up" before the "jump" from one domain of reality (from the rational reality of the form) — into its other domain (to the irrational reality of the content), i.e., from one (rational) component of the relation — to the other (irrational) component of this same relation. Thus, the number e is such a special quantitative correlation between the components in the relation-form {change/basis}, which answers to the limitpossible contentful filling (or the highest contentfulness) of this form-relation. At that, the irrationality of the number e testifies to the principled impossibility of the establishment in an exhaustive form of the final numerical correlation between the components of the relation-form {change/basis}, corresponding to its maximally contentful filling, and thereby to the impossibility of the acquisition of the highest contentfulness by this form-relation. The impossibility of the exhaustive expression of the highest contentfulness by the indicated form-relation is ascertained in the course of the modeling of the process of the acquisition (attainment) of this highest contentfulness by the specific procedure of the accrual of the compound bank interest with the unlimited shortening of the intervals of time between the moments of its accrual. As the experience of observations shows, in the descriptions of optimally proceeding real complex processes (with the maximal for definite conditions contentful increment) there are always present "e-forms," indicating thereby the belonging of the forms of these general complex processes to the number of optimal forms. On this score in the scientific literature there are presented numerous examples of the descriptions of the forms of the most contentful development of all sorts of processes in the most diverse domains of reality [9, 10, 11, 12, 17, 20, 29, 30, 31, 33, 36, 38, 41, 43, 46, 49, 55]. § 4. Having determined ourselves with the nature, meaning, and necessity of the introduction by man of the world constants into practical circulation, let us make yet several more clarifications and explanations As it has been possible to establish in the course of the investigation, all three constants examined by us — π, φ, and e — are limits, toward which functions-relations of a certain kind strive with the unlimited increase of their arguments. The historical excursus allows revealing the circle of particular practical tasks, in the course of the solution of which the first mentions of the above-named values appeared. The solutions of the indicated tasks are

 





 

  

connected with the finding of the algorithms of the computation of the following limits: in the case of the constant π — of the limit of the relation of the increasing perimeters of the polygons inscribed in the circumference (or circumscribed around it) to the diameter of this circumference [42, p. 43]; in the case of the constant φ — of the limit of the relation of the increasing total whole to its parts on the basis of the modeling of the computational procedure by means of the ascending sequence of Fibonacci numbers [39]; in the case of the constant e — of the limit of the relation of the content accreting in a definite way (up to "saturation") onto the content of the initial form (i.e., of the "numerator") to the content of the initial form (i.e., to the "denominator") on the basis of the modeling of the procedure of the computation of the compound bank (or usurious) interest [31]. The further analysis gives the possibility to discern the relations of the general sides-components of reality hiding behind the relations of the particular sides of the practical tasks: in the case of the constant π such sides of reality are the components of the relation {curvature/rectilinearity}, as well as the components of a series of other "kindred" relations: {continuity/discreteness}, {boundlessness/limitedness}, etc.; in the case of the constant φ — the components of the relation {whole/part}, as well as the components of a series of other similar relations: {general/singular}, {function/structure}, and the like; in the case of the constant e — the components of the relation {change/basis}, as well as the components of a series of other similar relations: {motion/rest}, {energy/mass}, {time/space}, etc. A scrupulous study helps to understand that behind the above-indicated constants there hide ways or devices of the expression and representation of the objective, but not quite comprehensible for man (irrational) sides of reality through the no less objective and considerably more comprehensible for man (rational) sides of it. In this sense the constants examined by us prove to be for human consciousness peculiar "transitions" or "bridges" from the domains of the rational — into the domains of the irrational: in the case of the constant π — this is the transition from "rectilinearity" to "curvature"; in the case of the constant φ — this is the transition from the "part" to the "whole"; in the case of the constant e — this is the transition from the "basis" to the "change." The above-mentioned limits are precisely the personification of the barriers, which must be overcome in order to "get over" from one domain of the relation into its other domain, from one component of reality to its other component. The infinite non-periodic string of digits of the fractional parts of the irrational numbers π, e, φ (and they are known today already with

billions of significant digits after the decimal point [2]) looks like an unending "run-up" before the "jump" from one domain of reality into another and simultaneously indicates the impossibility and unrealizability of the smooth overcoming of this barrier — the latter is overcome only by a leap in thinking, i.e., at the level of the notions and representations about the interacting contradictory sides-components of reality in their relations. Being expressed by irrational numbers, the given constants symbolize not simply transitions from one component of reality to another in their actual relations, but transitions infinite, uncompleted, unfinished, unclosed... Precisely this circumstance allows making one of the most important conclusions: if π, φ, e and can be called numbers in reliance on the established terminology with respect to the definition of an irrational number, then "constants" one cannot call them in any way! The symbols π, φ, e designate "living," uncompleted transitional processes, by which, strictly speaking, is caused the use in formulaic expressions and descriptions of any and all real forms and processes precisely of symbols, and not of concrete numbers. These are special forms of the expression of specific transitional contentful processes, which it would be proper to name precisely so: "π-transition," "φtransition," "e-transition" or "π-form," "φ-form," "e-form," — proceeding from the corresponding understanding and handling of them as of the designations of actual transitional forms and processes. The mathematical form of the expression of such limit transitions is the function. In the great multitude of descriptions of other all-possible formsprocesses (i.e., in the diverse formulaic expressions) these functions are present not as constants, but as forms of processes, in principle not having the possibility of being expressed by constant finite values; in the final analysis π, φ, and e — these are infinite and unrealizable to the end transitions from the rational to the irrational, clothed in mathematical form. As soon as and when these transitions are reduced to numbers with a limited quantity of significant digits after the decimal point, only then do they cease to be both processes, and transitions, and functions inside the descriptions of more complex and composite processes; only then do these forms turn into constants, the presence of which instead of the corresponding symbols limits the contentful potential (fullness and depth) of the mentioned descriptions. Thus, under the designations π, φ, and e there are implied "folded" algorithms (modeling the actual transitional processes) of the computation of the limit numerical values of the correlations of the components in the relations {curvature/rectilinearity}, {whole/part}, {change/basis} respectively. Besides, in the course of the examination it was ascertained that all the irrational numbers π, φ, e examined above designate limits or limit correlations of the components in their relations, answering to the highest contentfulness of the relations of these components. If one imagines that the

mentioned relations {curvature/rectilinearity}, {whole/part}, {change/basis} can be characterized by all-possible values of the correlations of their components in the ranges (determining the measure of the contentfulness of these correlations) from the conditional "zero" (i.e., of small contentfulness) up to some highest contentfulness, then the limit numerical values of the "round π," the "golden φ," the "saturated e" will correspond precisely to the maximal contentfulness of the above-enumerated relations (which, as has been noted more than once, in itself is likewise in principle unattainable and representable only in the form of an unattainable limit). Observations and practical experience show that by no means all formsrelations (objects, processes, phenomena) belong to the number of the "round," "golden," or "saturated." The overwhelming majority of the real "ordinary" forms-processes-relations occupies the most diverse steps of contentfulness within the limits of the ranges indicated above; true, all these "ordinary" forms-relations-processes likewise have as the directionality of their development the attainment of all the same heightened (and in the limit expression — the highest) contentfulness. But the main thing in this connection is nevertheless that among the boundless diversity of formsrelations man has managed to single out such which are characterized by heightened contentfulness, which is precisely what distinguishes these formsrelations from all the rest; at that, to the singled-out forms-relations it has been possible to put into correspondence the limit correlations of the components of these relations established by man in the form of more or less concrete numbers, which subsequently became the indicators of the heightened contentfulness of the given forms-relations. The singling-out and advancement of such forms from all the rest was and is accompanied by a burst of human emotions (admiration, tenderness, and delight), which in reality prove to be a reaction by no means to the internal heightened contentfulness of these forms in comparison with others, but to their external signs, characteristics, and properties. And the matter here, perhaps, is not even in the values and magnitudes of the numbers π, φ, and e themselves, but in the fact that such special numbers exist and they correspond to special (standing out from the rest) relations-forms of the development of reality. As has already been noted, the symbols π, φ, e designate the "boundary" correlations of the components in the most contentful specific formsrelations-processes. But these are a kind of particular cases among the multitude of other more general forms, the development of which is described by the generalized algorithm (formulation) of the principle of selfdevelopment. Therefore the presence of the symbols π, φ, e in the description of other more general complex processes and forms indicates the optimality and heightened effectiveness of these other complex forms and processes in comparison with the configurations of all the other real processes and forms. Besides, in the presence of objectively taking place ruptures or steps between the sides of reality at the boundaries of their division, in a series of cases the adequate description of really developing

forms and processes without the attraction of the "π-," "φ-," and "e-forms" is simply impossible. The following assertion will be the answer to the question posed at the beginning of the article: all three algorithms for the calculation of the limit correlations of π, φ, e — these are particular cases of the generalized algorithm (formulation) of the principle of self-development, which lies precisely in the basis of the fundamental regularity of development, conditioning the necessity of the seeking-out and practical application of such "constants." In all three cases the speech goes about conditionally "ideal," conditionally "exceptional," limit-contentful forms-relations, in some way isolated (especially in the case of the "e-form") from the interaction with all the other real forms. In the "harsh" reality of such exceptional (ideal) forms there are very few, they are — special; the forms of the open real world, however, are the product and consequence of the confrontation and interaction of the whole host of the forms of the open world. And with respect to such a kind of open real forms and processes the speech is conducted already not about their absolute perfection, exceptionality, and ideality (as in the case of the particular specific forms-relations), but about their movement-development in the direction of a certain optimality (i.e., of the maximally attainable contentfulness), but under the conditions of the real interference and intervention of other forms. And in the same way the sign of the orientation toward optimality and the highest contentfulness, but already in the case of the real complex open processes-forms, is the presence of the small "π-," "φ-," and "e-forms" in the descriptions of these big real open processes-forms. Thus, π, φ, and e — these are the algorithms of the particular specific formsrelations, characterized by the expressed directionality toward the attainment of the absolute perfection and exceptionality of their special forms, whereas the algorithm (formulation) of the principle of self-development determines the universal directionality of the movement-development of any all-possible forms-relations of reality, including also the specific particular forms investigated by us. Any and every SOMETHING is a relation, accessible to expression with respect to everything else and in mathematical or digital form. The number in the given case acts as the measure of any and every relation; the number is the quantitative characteristic of the relation, indicating the correlation of the components in their integral relation, the degree of the participation of the sides-components of the relation in one another, the degree of the expressiveness of one constituent by means of its other, the position of the boundary of division between the sides-components in their relation. The known examples of the found remarkable limits of π, φ, and e speak of the real and quite realizable possibility of the numerical expression (cognition) of one through the other: of the whole through the part (or through the totality of parts), of the boundless continuity through the limited discreteness, of the developing content (change) through the existing form (basis), of the

uncertainty through the certainty, of the unknown through the known (but every time with some differences from the already known), etc., etc. Precisely in such a way (by way of the expression of the unknown or of the contentful not "graspable" by consciousness in full measure through the known or cognized form-like) is realized the process of cognition (as, incidentally, also development itself), and another way and other path simply does not exist. The numbers π, φ, and e — these are examples of the resultative attempts of man to express one (insufficiently cognized) through the other (sufficiently comprehended). However, besides the load — to be "bridges" between the sides-components of reality — the so-called "constants" examined by us fulfill yet one more important function: all three "constants" — these are boundary pillars (milestones), standing on different frontiers and outlining the boundaries of the objective physical level of reality; the first two (π and φ) are located on the boundaries of the division of the domains connected with the external, for the most part static geometric (spatial) forms, the last one (e) — on the boundary of the division of the components of the more abstract, more deeplying and dynamic (temporal) forms-relations. Further, taking into account that π, φ, and e are located on the boundaries of the components of completely differently planned and differently leveled (in any case, obviously not intersecting) forms-relations, which are the relations-forms {curvature/rectilinearity}, {whole/part}, and {change/basis}, one must seek the quantitative connection between these forms-relations (and, correspondingly, between the numbers answering to them) with the account of this differently-leveledness, i.e., more meaningfully. It is not excluded that with time such an internal connection will be established or there will be discovered a system of numeration (and maybe, also a system of reference), in which the world "constants," like the chemical elements of the Mendeleev table, will in an ordered way occupy definite little cells of the table, thanks to which it will prove possible to forecast the appearance and introduction (dictated by practical necessity) into circulation of new world "constants." As for the other world "constants," and in particular the so-called physical "constants," then, probably, in a similar way one can investigate and establish the genuine essence both of the speed of light [4, pp. 98–107; 5, p. 37], and of the gravitational constant, and of the Planck constant, etc. At that, it is necessary to remember that every world "constant," still insufficiently comprehended and for the time being passing for an inexplicable and unsolved number, — this is always a barrier or at least a "boundary pillar" on the frontier between the incompatible and differing from one another components-constituents of a certain relation, i.e., between the objective sides of reality. Moreover, it is no less important also to understand that the mentioned relations of the noncoinciding and opposing sides of reality are not located somewhere there, in the "far distance" from one another, and are not separated between themselves by some

"spaces" and "times," but are interwoven into one another and are found in a single bundle in every smallest element of reality, occupying its different levels-floors. The "constants" have an important significance, since they stand on the frontiers between the thoroughly cognized and the insufficiently studied or not yielding to understanding also as easily and freely as the already cognized; precisely the world "constants" outline the boundaries of the rational (from the position of the stereotyped human thinking) world, i.e., of the habitual and very "definiteness-like" objective (geometrically-physical) level of reality. If π is located on the boundary of the transition from the linear (flat) world to the spherical (filled with curvature), then the number e and a whole series of other world "constants" stands on the threshold of the transformation of again the habitual for us three-dimensional world (absolutizing its static spatial-form side-constituent) into the dynamically changing, so-called space-time continuum. In view of the importance of the relations fixed by the designations of the world "constants," with each of these relations and the correlation of their components one must deal unhurriedly and carefully; offhand such questions cannot be correctly resolved. In any case one must begin with the study of the history of the origin of this or that problem form-relation — here at once there may be discovered the main hint for the disclosure of the secret of its special properties and, correspondingly, popularity. The following of the principle of the observance of the unity of the historical and the logical in the conduct of such examinations — is especially topical. The understanding of the essence of the world "constants" pushes apart the horizons of our representations about the developing reality, since the "constants" are the indispensable participants of the correct modeling of the behavior of the most multi-leveled reality, being found in the composition of the mathematical (formulaic) expressions describing this behavior. In this connection it is most expedient, in our opinion, to adhere to that position that with respect to each concrete case the best solution, interpretation, and explanation will be found by the "narrow" professional, who has mastered the concept of development presented here and mentioned yet again more than once. Say, the specialist in the domain of the history of the formation of geometry (with the knowledge both of mathematics and of its history, well acquainted moreover with the indicated concept of development) will find it easier than anyone else to come out onto the initial conditions and circumstances which led to the empirical or intuitive discovery of the remarkable correlation of π; or the physicist (well acquainted with the historical background and again with the given concept) will find it simpler to deal with the number e; and the biologist (zoologist, botanist), who has mastered the concept of development, — with the regularities of the golden relation in living forms, etc., etc. The world "constants" are waiting for their researchers.

In conclusion let us note that the present article serves as an example of the practical application of the concept of development as an instrument for the investigation of the surrounding world. Conclusion Let us sum up what has been set forth. 1. The world "constants" — these are the external forms of the manifestation of the internal connections and relations of the sides-components of reality; these are peculiar "bridges" between the mentioned sides-components in their real relations; these are the limit correlations of the definite sidesconstituents of reality, expressed by numerical values. 2. The concrete examples of the remarkable limits of π, φ, e testify to the principled realizability and resultatively realized by man aspirations and attempts of the quantitative expression (let it be even approximate) of the insufficiently cognized (irrational) sides-constituents of reality through the comprehensible and habitual (rational) sides-constituents of it. 3. The irrationality (i.e., the infinity and non-periodicity of the fractional parts) of the numbers corresponding to the mentioned limits of the relations of π, φ, and e indicates, in the first place, the principled unattainability of the expression in an exhaustive form of the irrational side-constituent of reality through the rational side-constituent of it and, in the second place, the possibility of an arbitrarily close approximation to such an exhaustive expression. 4. The same irrationality of the numbers π, φ, and e indicates also the fact that these numbers — by no means "constants," as is customary to consider, but certain forms of infinite processes of the transition from one sidecomponent of reality to its other side-component. Perceiving this at the level of intuition, man for that very reason includes them in the formulaic expressions and descriptions of real forms and processes not in the form of concrete numerical values, but in the form of symbols. 5. The world "constants" — these are a kind of "boundary markers" along the perimeter of the boundary of the most studied by man, comprehensible for him and habitual objective (physical) level of reality, i.e., precisely the world "constants" outline the boundaries of the so-called rational (from the position of the everyday human consciousness) world. 6. The limits of the relations of π, φ, and e — these are also "milestones" on the boundaries of the division between the components of quite differently leveled relations, such as: {curvature/rectilinearity}, {whole/part}, {change/basis}; for that very reason to seek the quantitative correlations between their numerical values without the meaningful penetration into these relations and their components preceding the given search, in our

opinion, is little effective. It is quite admissible that between the aboveenumerated forms-relations there exists a deep-lying contentful (nonformal) connection, which may be discovered with time; but in order to speak about this concretely, further in-depth investigations of these very relations and their components are needed. 7. The numerical values of π, φ, and e designate the correlations of the components in the above-enumerated forms-relations, answering to the heightened (and in the limit — the highest) contentfulness of these formsrelations on their levels, which makes these forms-relations the "happy" possessors of their remarkable properties and characteristics, fixed by the human consciousness as something special and exceptional. 8. Since, without resorting to the help of the "constants" investigated by us, many real processes it is simply impossible to describe correctly and adequately, man attracts these specific forms-relations (π, φ, and e) for participation in the descriptions of many other real complex forms and processes. Being present as the designations of the processes of transition in the formulaic expressions and descriptions of other, more complex forms and processes, these specific forms-relations of heightened contentfulness indicate thereby the optimality of those other forms and processes in comparison with the multitude of other "ordinary" forms and processes, not distinguished by a similar optimality. Besides, under the designations of the "constants" in such descriptions there hide compromise connections (bridges), "smoothing" the objectively existing ruptures or steps between the components of reality. The Structure of the Musical Scale [Text]. — 2nd ed. — M.: Nauka, 1980. — 24 p. 49. Schrödinger E. The Space-Time Structure of the Universe [Text] / Transl. from English; ed. by R.A. Asanov. — M.: Nauka; Chief ed. of phys.-math. lit., 1986. — 224 p. 50. Steinhaus H. The Universal Criterion of Truth

The Universal Criterion of Truth Abstract The cornerstone of the epistemology of any philosophical system or doctrine is the question of truth. Based on the analysis of the work of Vladimir I. Lenin "Materialism and Empiriocriticism", it is shown that the criterion of truth or of the truthfulness of the forms of developing reality is not practice, erroneously considered as such to this day, but the contentfulness (substantive richness, pithiness) of these forms; practice, in its turn, is a method or procedure of evaluating the fullness or the "quantity" of the contentfulness of the forms of reality. Moreover, practice represents only a particular case of a more developed technology of establishing the truth — interpretation. The criterion of truth put forward in the present article makes it possible to evaluate quantitatively the significance of the forms of reality, which, in its turn, gives the possibility to rank the forms and, on this basis, to outline more meaningfully the directions of promising investigations. In the same connection it is noted that there do not exist two or more equally contentful truths (ideas, representations, statements, theories, concepts, models, opinions, points of view, etc.) with respect to one and the same investigated question or subject of judgment, just as there cannot in principle exist two or more equivalent approximations to objective truth (absolute truth). At every given moment of development, the truth in the indicated sense is always one. In addition, it is shown that the concept of "reflection" ("copy", "mapping") of reality, used by dialectical materialism and other philosophical doctrines for the description of the principle of the functioning of human thinking or consciousness, serves as a limitation or barrier on the path of understanding the essence of the processes of development and cognition of reality; objective reality represents the movement and change of forms in the

direction of the possession of the highest contentfulness (i.e., of the maximally achievable content), and all processes of development and cognition of reality are to be studied and considered through the prism of content-form transitions, conversions, transformations of unitary directionality. § 1. Questions of the theory of cognition In October 1908, Vladimir Ilyich Lenin finished, on the whole, the work on the book "Materialism and Empiriocriticism". A good half of this work is devoted to the critical analysis of the theory of cognition of empiriocriticism (a subjectively idealistic trend in philosophy, fashionable at the beginning of the XX century) and to the exposition of views on the same questions of the theory of cognition from the position of dialectical materialism (for brevity: diamat) [11]. Beginning with antiquity and up to the present time, the "central category" of the theory of cognition and the "most important, irreplaceable concept of science" is considered to be the category of truth [4, p. 6]. The ancient dispute of philosophers concerning the cognizability of the world and the truthfulness of the knowledge obtained, extracted, acquired, it would seem, was resolved there, in this Lenin's work, in general outline in favor of the point of view of the Marxists, to whose number the author himself belonged [19, p. 352—353]. And nevertheless, again and again generations of investigators of the surrounding world come, who do not tire of "stepping on the same rake" and continue to fill their own bumps at the price of their own delusions. Thus, the long-standing philosophical dispute does not subside to this day, and, in all probability, a more complete solution of the same problem, as always, is impossible outside a fresh look at the established state of affairs and without reliance on a worldview requiring periodic refinement. With regard to the indicated considerations, the present work (article) is called upon: a) to show that the representations of diamat about truth in the main are nevertheless more correct and stand closer to the truth than the representations of other philosophical systems and doctrines; b) and then, already on this basis, to develop and supplement somewhat the representations of diamat. The first question, actual at all times, considered by V.I. Lenin in his book on behalf of dialectical materialism and in connection with the mentioned analysis of the theory of cognition, is the question of the principled cognizability of the surrounding world in the course of the acquisition of knowledge about it.

The second question, following from the first, is the question of the criteria of truthfulness, reliability, authenticity of the forms of the acquired knowledge. The first question is resolved in the work of V.I. Lenin positively by the recognition as real of a series (chain) of objective "contents" or the so-called relative truths, represented by different cognitive forms, as well as by the indication of the movement and change of these forms or relative truths in the course of cognition in the direction of the achievement of absolute truth. The positive solution of the second question is connected with the reasonable (rational) sufficiency of the criterion of practice (as the criterion of truth), which is "so 'indefinite' as not to allow man's knowledge to turn into an 'absolute', and at the same time so definite as to wage a merciless struggle against all varieties of idealism and agnosticism" [11, p. 138]. § 2. How diamat answers the questions of the theory of cognition Let us dwell in more detail on how dialectical materialism answers the questions about the possibility of cognition (i.e., of the cognizability) of the surrounding world and about the evaluation of the truthfulness of the acquired knowledge. From the position of diamat there exists an external world or object, containing in itself some objective content, on the one hand, and a subject cognizing (sensing and realizing) this world, on the other hand. The fact that the diverse forms of the surrounding world (objects, bodies, things, processes, phenomena, etc.) are bearers of a certain objective content, independent of the cognizing subject, raises no doubt from the point of view of diamat, since the results of the millennial practical activity of man testify to this. The question remains — how correctly the representations of the cognizing subject about these forms of the surrounding world correspond to the content of these forms independent of the subject. In the words of V.I. Lenin: "The objects of our representations differ from our representations, the thing in itself differs from the thing for us, for the latter is only a part or one side of the former, just as man himself is only a small particle of the nature reflected in his representations." But if for Kant the "thing in itself" is an "abstraction without reality", then for Feuerbach the "thing in itself" is an "abstraction with reality", i.e., the world existing outside us is quite cognizable and in nothing principally different from the "phenomenon" [11, p. 115]. Further, the presence of objective content in the feelings, sensations, and perceptions (i.e., in the "experience" — according to the terminology widespread at that time [11, p. 123, 327]) of the cognizing subject is sought out and confirmed. And, finally, the "objective content... of concepts, ideas, judgments, theories, doctrines, and of the integral picture of the world in the dialectics of its development" [19, p. 226] is established, i.e., the presence of objective

content in the abstract forms (products) of human thinking is proved. The fact that objective content must be present and is present in the abstract forms and products of thinking of the cognizing subject is, from the position of diamat, again beyond doubt. The question reduces only to the manner in which this can be confirmed. In other words, in order to establish the truthfulness of the obtained or acquired knowledge (judgments, statements, theories, concepts, formulations of laws and regularities, doctrines, paradigms, models, etc.), it is necessary again to understand — whether the given knowledge (more precisely, its subjectively ideal forms) is likewise a bearer of objective content. § 3. A review of the existing criteria of the truthfulness of knowledge The ruler of today's representations about the criteria of the evaluation of the authenticity or reliability of knowledge occupies the range from vulgarobjective objectivism to extremely idealistic subjectivism. Let us enumerate briefly the most known and vivid of these representations [7]. Objectivism considers the phenomena of reality as historically necessary irrespective of the subject of activity and cognition, and therefore truth here is almost identified with objectivity [13, p. 418]. In other words, the speech here goes, as it were, not even about knowledge (a theoretical-abstract form), the truthfulness or reliability of which is necessary to establish, but about representations identical with or "coinciding" with the object itself. The founders of the already mentioned empirio-criticism considered that the correctness or truthfulness of thinking is conditioned by the principles of the "economy of thinking" (according to E. Mach) and of the "least expenditure of forces" (according to R. Avenarius) [11, p. 165-167]; these same principles they laid also into the foundation of their theory of cognition. R. Descartes assumed clarity and obviousness as the criterion of truth: if something is understood clearly and obviously, then it is true. By different thinkers there were proposed as criteria of truth, for example, the internal consistency of thoughts, their value, etc. Pragmatism puts forward the following criterion: true is that which is useful or advantageous to people. By the flows of information of modern mass media there is unprecedentedly agitated and imposed on man the justice of the following point of view with respect to the evaluation or choice of the criterion of truth: whoever is stronger (whoever wins) — that one is right. In other words, if one succeeds by force, by pressure, by "impudence", by unexampled insolence, cunning, and rigging to push through and implant into the consciousness of some multitude of people (laymen) any doubtful judgment, statement, conclusions, or inference (and in the limiting case — even a whole system of world-

understanding), then the result of such an action is identified with truth or verity; in many cases it is precisely such a path that is chosen today by different subjects for the assertion of the rightness of their position or point of view. A number of known philosophers see the criterion of truth in "universal significance". From the point of view of this criterion, truth is that which is "universally significant", i.e., that with which all people agree, or the overwhelming majority of them, or simply the majority. A variety of this point of view is yet another one, which was adhered to by the French mathematician A. Poincaré. According to the concept put forward by him (this is the so-called conventionalism), scientific concepts and theories do not reflect objective reality, but are the result of an agreement of scientists or arbitrarily established rules. The quintessence of his views is expressed by the following phrase: "The only true objective reality is the internal harmony of the world" [11, p. 161]. Kindred to the views of A. Poincaré there exists also such a point of view: the criterion of truth is beauty (as a manifestation of the internal and external harmony of the world). It must be said that the variant of appointing as the criterion of truth the "universally significant" (generally accepted, generally obligatory, etc.) is analyzed in detail by V.I. Lenin in this same book of his, where it is shown that in many cases the "universally significant" is a synonym or equivalent of collective human delusions, if this "universally significant" is deprived of objective content, given to man in experience, i.e., acquired or established by the empirical path in the course of the cognition of the surrounding world (or, what is the same, in the course of the object-practical activity of man). § 4. The subjectivism of the majority of the criteria of truth It is not difficult to see that the majority of the above-enumerated criteria is, to one degree or another, imbued with the spirit of subjectivism. With all the importance of the signs or indicators of the truthfulness of knowledge contained in them, there is absent the main one, which would emphasize or point to the objectivity of knowledge, i.e., would tie it to the objectivity of the content of the forms of the external world. Certainly, no more or less integral or balanced philosophical system (doctrine) will consider the truthfulness of knowledge outside the connection with objective reality, and in the first place — with that objective world in which such a system abides (whether it "wants" it or not). Hence grows the need for a complex criterion which would establish and take into account the presence of that very objective content in all abstract and subjective forms of knowledge. To this day, such a more or less balanced system of views is recognized to be the dialectical-materialistic philosophy,

which does not deny the significance of the clarity of thoughts, their consistency, their universal significance, their rationality, economy, obviousness, usefulness, pragmaticality, harmony, etc., etc., but at the same time is fully aware of the fact that the above-enumerated indicators (signs), because of the absence of a sufficiently solid and stable natural connection of theirs with the external world, cannot be recognized as fundamental criteria of truth. Proceeding from such an understanding, dialectical materialism sees as the most suitable sought-for criterion of truth practice or the objectpractical activity of man as a subject of cognition. Thus, under the vaults of the soundly built edifice of dialectical-materialistic philosophy, as its core there is discovered a seemingly quite harmonious system of views on the fundamental questions of the theory of cognition. § 5. Peculiarities of the interpretation by diamat of the nature of the subjectively ideal forms of cognition (forms of the representation of knowledge) And here I would like to draw attention to one peculiarity of the just mentioned system of views. If the subjectively idealistic philosophy turns the sensations of the subject cognizing the external world into a certain "partition, wall separating consciousness from the external world", then contrary to these representations diamat considers that "sensation is indeed the direct connection of consciousness with the external world, is the transformation of the energy of external irritation into a fact of consciousness" [11, p. 49]. As we see, according to the views of diamat, in defiance of the views criticized by it of the idealistic (it should be said, both subjectively and objectively idealistic) doctrines, no barrier separating the objective (including the three-dimensional world of objects habitual for us) world, firstly, from the sensual world (sensations, experience) and, secondly, from the ideal world of the cognizing subject, seemingly does not exist. We look further: "These facts of consciousness are not merely some conventional signs, symbols, hieroglyphs, but represent copies, images of real things and processes taking place in nature, and cognition itself is a dialectical process of the reflection by consciousness of the objective world" [19, p. 352]. In other words, we see that the level of sensations here is still recognized as the "direct connection of consciousness with the external world". But what about consciousness itself? — and here it is, it has turned into a "fact", and the facts of consciousness represent already certain "copies", "images", "reflections" of real things and natural processes. According to these representations, the objective content of thinking exists in reality only in the form of a copy, in the form of some reflection of reality. At first, it would seem, one got rid of one "partition" between the forms of objectivity and the forms of thinking, but exactly to the extent of then coming to another "partition" — cognition turns into a dialectical process of

the reflection by consciousness of the objective world. Thereby an involuntary start is given to the process of the "multiplication of entities", with which even a thoroughly religious consciousness cannot agree: willynilly there comes to mind the saying of the English monk known under the name of "Occam's razor": "Entities should not be multiplied without necessity." Denying in words the idealistic doctrines with their religious core, in fact we go into religion, for every religion is precisely the earthly embodiment (reflection, copying "in image and likeness") of some unearthly entity. Marxism, diamat, and indeed any materialism of any worth stand on the positions of the rigid opposition and separation by a "partition" or "wall" of everything objective and empirical (material) from everything ideal, which represents a certain ephemeral "copy" or "reflection" of everything material (read: real). At that, into what category this very "reflection" should be assigned, nobody still knows properly: on "this side" it does not take root in any way (for it is not material), whereas "otherworldliness" is ridiculed and rejected by the materialistic doctrines in the most resolute and merciless manner. The presence of such an inconsistency (according to the terminology given above — a "partition" or "wall") violates, in our opinion, the harmonious system of representations about the transformation or transfer of objective content from form to form in the process of cognition and leaves no chances for the correct interpretation and correct forecasting of the direction of further transformations of the forms of cognition.
