Meanwhile, in the course of the accumulation of knowledge and the improvement of representations about the world surrounding us, the circumstance is realized ever more distinctly and clearly that our environment, just as we ourselves, is a changing, moving, developing multilevel reality, "being made" and "reproducing itself" in the real-time mode and manifesting itself at every moment in a single, unrepeatable, unique copy. In such an aspect, the well-known to us sayings about a certain "copying", "photographing", "mapping" and in the general case — "reflection" of objectively existing reality [11, p. 125] — somehow no longer "get along" very well with the actual state of affairs. § 6. Description of the process of the "ascent" of contentfulness by successive forms But, perhaps, one should not approach the interpretation of the process of cognition, as well as the perception and interpretation of the motion, development, evolution of nature, man, and society, so straightforwardly and "head-on", so mechanistically and trivially? Why not consider this same process from a somewhat different position or in a somewhat different light, namely: some deep essence or objective content

of the developing reality "ascends" and manifests itself in separate successive and mutually conjugated concrete forms — by the conventionallyinitial form of thingness (the world of things), further by the form of sensations (the vegetable and animal world, including man) and, finally, by the form of thinking (subjective abstract human thinking). Being objective content, initially represented by the thing form (about any initiality of representation one can speak here very conditionally, since about the forms preceding the thing ones, including various physical fields, we cannot yet say anything definite here at all), the mentioned contentfulness in the process of its motion-development changes the form for a more flexible and capacious one, which turns out to be, from the point of view of human consciousness, the form of sensations, which then, in its turn, is replaced by a form still more capacious and embracing, and therefore more abstracted, called by us the form of thinking. For this reason, each of the indicated forms, as merely different shells of the same single, but transforming at the transition from level to level, objective content, must easily conjugate with the forms preceding and following it, which would testify to the succession (and, in the final analysis, to the truthfulness) of all these forms. The objective content independent of the subject (by analogy with the above-mentioned "thing in itself"), present in any and every object of the surrounding world, "ascends" (like the "ascent" of some deep essence of the surrounding world) first into the subjective and abstract contentful forms of the vegetable and animal world (including the form of human experience, to which the sensuous, or sensations, belongs) and then — into the ever more abstract forms of human thinking. In other words, the process "begins" to develop from the moment of the conditional start of the most "coarse", mechanistic-materialistic thing forms, further continues with the forms endowed with sensuousness (to which belong the sensations and reactions of the living — the vegetable and animal world), then is "taken over" by the forms-carriers of the images of consciousness and representations already in combination with the forms-carriers of sensations and the forms-carriers of thingness (higher animals and "early" man) and further continues with the forms-carriers of ever more abstract intellectual-spiritual contentfulness (which are "late", i.e., modern man and society) again in combination with all the preceding forms. Thanks to the ability to conjugate the separate forms of the single "ascending" objective content, following one after another as continuations of the preceding forms, there arises a generalized (aggregate) multilevel form (of man with his thinking), consisting, like a pyramid, of interconnected and unimaginably interwoven layers-levels-forms, the summit part of which (of the pyramid) is crowned by the form-carrier of an extremely abstract (the most abstracted of those reliably known to us) content (theoretical knowledge).

Having before our eyes the just drawn image of the pyramid, it remains only to make sure that the contents of the mutually linked layers-forms-levels of this pyramid are indeed successive, and, consequently, objective, i.e., represent separate links of the single objective content of reality developing in a through manner (by means of the above-indicated forms). The latter, in its turn, is proved with the help of a very well-known procedure, from time immemorial called practice (about which we will speak much and in detail below). Thus, the "hidden essence" of reality being revealed, or its objective content, "marches" in a through manner in the process of its ascent-development by means of different, growing one out of another and therefore mutually linked forms. The distinct visibility and confident ascertainment of this circumstance is hindered by the closest interweaving of the forms of the motion of objective content passing one into another. At the same time, one can notice that, being represented by different forms, the fragments of this general objective content differ from one another by some of their peculiarities, which are introduced into them in the course of their development from the initial objective, but still to some extent, uncertainty. So, in accordance with our representations, separate objects (bodies, things, objects) are the forms of existence and "acquiring" of the objective content of reality at the level of the inanimate, manifested by means of the formslevels of the living, i.e., of "sensations" and "thinking". Further, plants and animals are the forms of the motion of that same objective content at the level and through the levels of the living inanimate, growing out of the forms (and being the continuation of the forms) of the inanimate and, like the preceding thing forms, expressed and manifested by means of the forms-levels of "sensations" and "thinking". Finally, man with his thinking (mankind and society) is an even more specific form of the motion of again that same objective content at the level and through the levels of subjective thinking (consciousness), connected even more closely by direct and feedback connection with the forms of the living and the inanimate and being the immediate continuation of the latter. This form is also expressed and manifested by means of the forms-levels of "sensations" and "thinking". And since the whole just enumerated set of forms is the continuation of the thing form, there is no need at that to introduce some intermediate gaskets in the form of the notions and terms "reflection" or "copy" only in order to emphasize once again the peculiar character of such forms. In other words, the chain of the above-enumerated forms is the same expression, manifestation, motion, "march", development of the objective content, only

in the most specific, called subjective, forms, which are the continuations of the preceding thing forms. The root of the erroneousness of the representations about such, in particular, a form as the form of thinking (as about some ephemeral formation — the "ideal reflection" of reality), and the problem of the interpretation of such a form lie in the artificial separation of the highest ("thinking") layer from the general single bundle or aggregate of all the levels-forms available in the "human" structure and the subsequent absolutization and opposition of it (of this most specific form) to all the preceding forms. Of course, it is undoubted that the objective contentfulness, which manifests itself and can manifest itself only through subjective forms, will never get rid of the shade of some subjectivity, but at the same time it remains objective by its nature, by its origin, by its roots, which lie in the foundation of the whole chain of forms ensuring the transformation of this objectivity into some specific subjectivity in the course of the motion-development of reality. The objective content simultaneously ascends through the subject, and moves in this form of its in the direction of the achievement of its "final" expression in this same or another more perfect form, as the most perfect of all the forms. The difference of the forms of the representation of the objective contentfulness in the course of its motion-development is conditioned and characterized by different values or magnitudes of the intensity of the contentful-formal transformations at different levels of the representation and acquiring of these forms. The change of the intensity of the contentful-formal transformations at the transition from level to level in the final analysis turns out to be the main factor determining the development of the forms of reality. [In this work this aspect is practically not considered and not disclosed.] By every fragment, episode, act of its existence objects (if the speech goes about the inanimate), by every breath and step an animal, by every deed or phrase man extract the hidden essence of their nature from inside outward and make it a phenomenon (make it visible), which (the phenomenon) in fact turns out every time to be only some next approximation to the full and all-round expression of this very essence. The subject with his consciousness does not "reflect" reality, i.e., does not duplicate and does not multiply entities, but continues by himself the search for a more contentful form (correcting and supplementing the contentfulnesses of the preceding forms-levels in their aggregate) of the reality developing in such a way. And this new sought-for refined form is realized as a superstructure over the preceding aggregate of forms, absorbing, including into itself in the main features, in a concentrated ("sublated") form the contents of the preceding forms. From the thing forms — to the subjectively ideal forms (which are simultaneously the constituent parts of the general aggregate of forms,

entering into "engagement" or conjugating with those same thing forms), — this is, in accordance with our representations, the direction of the development of reality at the present stage in the cluster of the forms known to us. Let us show and analyze the process of the "ascent" and transformation of the objective content by the example of a concrete object — a certain spherical boulder-stone, the definiteness of the form, dimensions, and internal structure of which forces one to "reckon" with these peculiarities of its: — firstly, the inanimate objects surrounding it, running into it under the influence of diverse external forces as into an objectively present obstacle and interacting with it as with an obstacle; — secondly, the moving living bodies, interacting with it and "taking into account" it already not only as an obstacle, but reproducing or generating in this connection some simplest abstract images of it; — thirdly, the living animate organisms, which along with the results of the thing interactions with it and the sensuous representations about it, manifest its essence also in the form of developed abstract images and representations, extending from the abstract formula description of its spherical surface to the descriptions of the physical, chemical, and quantummechanical properties of its material in the guise of even more abstracted symbolic-formula expressions, mathematical equations, and theoretical computations. By the example of a spherical boulder (such specimens are also encountered in nature), rounded from all sides by a glacier and subjected to the long-term influence of squall winds, stormy waters, raging fires, as well as of similar stone blocks (by virtue of collisions with the latter), one can trace the "ascent" of the contentful essence of a separate object by means of the forms changing in the course of such an "ascent". An object of finite, limited dimensions, fixed and imprinted ("perceived") by the neighboring objects as an obstacle, passes with time into the stage of the sensuous perception and the simplest figurative description of it by living organisms as streamlined, hard, solid, massive... and further passes to the stage of the perception of it by human thinking and the description by abstract means in the form of complicated images, symbols, and formulas: as a spherical object of radius R with the surface approximately calculated by the formula 4πR²; as an object with the volume approximately calculated by the formula (4/3)πR³; as an object with the ultimate strength of the material for rupture (or compression), calculated as the ratio of the ultimate admissible load on the object to the area of its cross-section; as an object with hardness determined in conventional units, say, by Brinell; as an object with wear resistance and other properties and characteristics determined by formulas and mathematical dependences known from the courses of mathematics, physics, chemistry, strength of materials... quantum mechanics, etc., etc. And all this complex description can be qualified as the

manifested contentful essence of one and the same something, represented by the forms of different degrees of abstractness and subjectivity, — in the course of the ascent of this essence successively through the forms-carriers of thingness, the forms-carriers of sensations and, finally, through the formscarriers of thinking. To express the strength and hardness of the material of the boulder through the forms of standards found by the empirical path and established at that, such as the limits of the strength of its material (say, for rupture and shear), and through the empirically established conventional units of hardness (say, by Rockwell, Brinell, or Vickers), — this means to find the expression of new abstract knowledge about the stone (i.e., to find the expression of its objective content, developing by new and ever more abstract forms) through the contents of the concrete thing and sensuous forms-standards formed or shaped "in experience". The same concerns the knowledge about the other properties of the stone, such, say, as wear resistance, density, thermal conductivity, electrical conductivity, etc., etc. Let us now return to the above-cited words of V.I. Lenin regarding the cognition of the world as a process of the sequential transformation of the "thing-in-itself" into the "thing-for-us", and see what emerges if we reinterpret Lenin's words in light of the concept of the "ascent" of the objective content of reality (its deep essence) through forms transforming from objective substantial to subjective abstract ones, which has just been expounded. Then, the initial Lenin quote with our addition will read approximately as follows: "The objects of our representations differ from our representations, the thing-in-itself differs from the thing-for-us, for the latter is only a part or one side of the former, just as man himself..." — is merely a continuation of the content-based development of the "thing-in-itself" into the "thing-for-us" through the aggregate of specific forms manifested by it, which grow sequentially one out of another, such as the form of sensations and the form of thought, which have transformed in the course of the aforementioned content-based development from the form of substantiality. In the course of the described "ascent" of the content-based component of developing reality — initially through non-living substantial forms, then through living forms of sensations, and further through the intellectual and spiritual forms of human thought — all the aforementioned forms emerge as products of the "demonstration" of the accumulating contentfulness, which products then manifest themselves in the form of successive technologies and ideologies. Reality, as can be seen from our examples, remains the same general, continuous, unified changing substance, but it manifests itself differently at different stages of its movement-development, that is, in different forms: from forms that are concrete and objective (independent of our consciousness, according to the assessment of our own consciousness) — to forms that are increasingly abstract and subjective...

Here it is very important to understand that there is no ready (once and for all) essence of the world, which somewhere "hides" from man and which man has only to cognize in small portions and steps, just as there is no ready (completely exhausting itself and existing independently of anything) objective world: the essence of the world "ascends" by contentful forms sprouting one out of another, thanks to which the objective world is "made" every moment, is recreated and reproduced from itself by the build-up of layer-by-layer changes on the available and simultaneously changing in such a way basis. The neighboring external object is not simply "reflected" in me (in my consciousness), but exists through me by its content, which changes together with me and my consciousness (as the continuation of the relation of the components {I/object} or of the more capacious relation {subject/object}) in the course of the deepening of the knowledge about this object. The essence of the changes taking place or occurring in the objective world "ascends" through me and by means of me, in direct connection or in relation with me (as well as in relation with another individual, and in relation with an animal, and in relation with an inanimate object). Without me this will be already a somewhat different form of the manifestation of the essence, and with another "I" — this will be a third form of the manifestation of the essence, etc. And only the generalized form as the aggregate of the aboveenumerated forms exposes, reproduces some generalized collective contentfulness, which only can be called the objective content. In other words, about a separate and independent of anyone or anything absolute essence there is nothing to speak here at all — such does not exist in nature. The essence is "extracted outward", i.e., manifests itself only in the process of the interaction of something one with something other. In accordance with such representations about development, the changeability of the forms (from the concrete forms of thingness to the abstract forms of thinking) corresponds to the habitual natural (from the position of human consciousness) course of events. Now, in the light of and on the basis of these representations, one can come to a more exact understanding and conclusion about the actual relation of the components of the subjective and the objective in the process of the described motion of the forms (from the thing ones — to thinking). Man is a multilevel formation, consisting of a hierarchical sequence (transformed with time into an inseparable aggregate) of conjugating formslevels characterized by their succession (it is precisely this that, first of all, should catch the eye, but is taken into account by few). The aggregate or bundle of levels, beginning from the atomic and molecular (forming the thing level), further — to the cellular, organ-tissue, and organismic (forming the animal level or the level of the "living inanimate") and further — to the biospheric, subjective, and social (forming the level of the "living animate

and socially organized"), — represents a specimen of the "cohesive" organization of the forms-levels transforming one into another and therefore contentfully successive (including in themselves also even more numerous subforms-sublevels). On the basis of the adduced reasonings one can come to the following, not unimportant, in our opinion, conclusion: the consciousness of man is not some passive "reflection" of reality, but it is reality itself, the reality itself or, more exactly, the next form of the manifestation of the latter at the next stage of its (of the reality) "ascent", development. At this stage of its motiondevelopment the reality appears before us, as well as before itself, in the specific form of man and his thinking. In this case, consciousness should be accepted not as a dead passive reflection or copy of everything preceding and existing inanimate and living, but as the next form of the manifestation of the contentfulness of the ascending, developing reality, continuing and uniting by itself the whole row of the preceding forms of this same reality. With such an approach, the living chain of development (and its logic) is not interrupted, growing by ever more capacious, contentful and therefore ever more abstracted links-forms. Such a research approach gets rid of the artificial restriction from above on any development and, in particular, liquidates the known barrier erected by diamat. The succession of the changing forms of reality is not blocked and does not end with the form of man and his thinking, but goes ever more in depth, into an ever more abstracting reality... Thereby the process of cognition is also not restricted to the comprehension of the form of thinking as the most "summit" form of the forms of reality reliably known to us so far, but presupposes the presence and appearance of even more developed forms of it with a greater intensity of the contentful-formal transformations (transformations). But, what is more essential, such an approach makes it possible to forecast the further course of development (and, correspondingly, of cognition) and to anticipate with an increasing degree of accuracy the appearance of new forms of the changing reality. § 7. A model of reality from the position of our representations. The principle of self-development But what, at least in the first and the roughest approximation, is this developing reality that serves as the subject of our discussion? Let us try to determine a more or less integral model of it, answering to our views. Let us imagine some "traveling" two-component, but single integral formation or construction of the kind {content/form} — a very abstract one (in its most general configuration), but in the course of its "travel" always appearing in a concrete form, changing in the direction or towards the side of an ever greater and greater predominance in the composition of this construction of the component "content" and the acquisition thereby by the

"numerator" of this relation of an ever greater specific weight within the scales of the integral relation [2, 8, 23]. The changeability of this abstract relation {content/form}, directed in a definite way, is conditioned by the simultaneous unity and non-identity (dissimilarity) of its components, by virtue of which both components are in a state of interaction and inexhaustible motion towards rapprochement with each other and the acquisition of equilibrium (balance) or mutual expressiveness by each other (which means: to become maximally contentful — for the form, and to determine itself with its perfect form — for the content). The presence in one integral relation of two components with different types of activity gives rise to a vortex, a whirlwind, a circulation, a "self-winding" spiral, i.e., a change (motion, development) of a specific character both of both constituents of this relation and of the relation itself as a whole. "Empirical experience" and abstract thinking make it possible to reveal the deep essence of this motion, clothing it in the formulation of a certain universal principle of self-development [2, p. 10; 8, p. 53-54; 23, p. 4]: the development of content is carried out in the direction of the exhaustive expression by the developing content of the very same developing content. Applied to the surrounding world, this means approximately the following: everything that exists and develops has as the direction of its development and existence the expression by itself of the existing and developing itself existing and developing. The abstract relation {content/form} with a quite unambiguous directedness of the change-motion-development of the components or constituents of this relation, as well as of the whole relation as a whole, — this is what exists initially, everywhere, objectively and independently of any concreteness whatsoever; this is what, by virtue of its omnipresence, permeates through itself all the available and arising concrete forms-levels-relations. It is precisely the contentful-formal transformations of the above-described directedness (i.e., the changes of the components of the relation in a strictly definite side, in a strictly definite direction) that lie in the foundation of the development of the forms of reality, which are, in their turn, merely the concrete attire and embodiment of the abstract model presented above. And it is precisely this initial model that, by virtue of its solidity, is, in our opinion, the most acceptable and effective for the in-depth study, research, and penetration into the essence of the surrounding world (the changing reality) at the present stage of its development. The noted dual-unity with a very strict and definite directedness of the contentful-formal transformations (transformations) explains the everymoment uniqueness of the world being acquired (the Universe, nature, life, etc.), as well as the uniqueness and diversity of the existing and nascent forms as separate elements of this world.

Being a strictly abstract formation, the indicated relation {content/form} is in principle not manifestable in its most general kind or image, but at the same time constitutes the core or the very essence of any and every real concrete form. The historical path of the abstract construction {content/form}, likened to the "travel" of it in concrete images, runs through a great multitude of all sorts of concrete forms (including also such kindred to it, but still more concrete forms-relations as {subject/object}, {subjective/objective}, {abstract/concrete}, etc.). It is not the task of the present article to trace the contentful-formal transformations of this relation in the course of its historical motion-development, — and therefore we shall note only that the initial, as, probably, and the final points of its "travel" are lost in the hazy mists of the past and the future, in parallel coexisting in a single bundle or aggregate of concrete forms growing one out of another and transforming in the course of the "travel" under consideration. Under the "control" of the mentioned principle of self-development, internally inherent in all formations-forms and conditioning the existence (on its basis) of all forms without exception, from the form of thingness, accepted by us as the conditional beginning of the development of all the other forms of the surrounding world, there developed the next new form of sensations, united with the first form into one whole. This second form differs from the first by a greater intensity of content-form transformations, that is, it ensures feedback, or return to itself, and the "cognition" (reproduction, self-organization) of itself with a greater "velocity" than the substantial form. Under the pressure and control of the same principle, the second form has developed over time into an even more capacious and, accordingly, more abstract form of thought, uniting within itself, as it were, three interrelated forms — the substantial, the sensory, and the cognitive; and this third form is characterized by an even greater intensity of content-form transformations in comparison with the two preceding forms. Thus, all three indicated forms "operate" as if in a single bundle, in a unified aggregate, ensuring an ever more intensive "unwinding" of the processes of development and cognition of the surrounding world under the "control" of the universal principle of self-development common to all forms. Man, who appears primarily as the "carrier" of the form of thought, in fact represents a tight knot of all three forms, characterized by the harmonious unity of these individual forms within their formed aggregate, crowned by the most abstracted, powerful, and capacious form of thought. Man is a "pyramid" of the above-listed interlinked forms of developing reality in the thus "equipped" next "advanced" form of the same developing reality. Under the "diktat" of the principle of self-development, the content of any form moves beyond its own form in the direction of possessing a form of even greater contentfulness, that is, a form of greater capacity and receptacle for the "accommodation" of this self-developing, "self-making" content. Thus, the process of the content "coiling" onto itself while existing — with the formation of

a hierarchical chain of forms that are ever more content-capacious and, therefore, ever more abstracted — is incessantly carried out. The very formulation of the principle of self-development indicates that the process of cognition is carried out by the expression or replacement of the incomprehensible by the comprehended, of the unknown by the known and well-familiar, i.e., by the circles of self-expression; and such a way of motion (cognition) most exactly fits the interpretation of the term "interpreting" (about which we will speak below). All the constructions of new forms, including the formation of the cognitive forms, go by the expression of the forms through themselves. In essence, the process of cognition, being at first a separate twig standing out on the general trunk of development, closes up in the further course with the latter, turning out to be simply its continuation identical to it. Since in nature there is no other building material than nature itself or the surrounding world, everything is built and superstructed from itself, by "selfmaking", — everything "dances from the stove", from the basis, by means of the unceasing self-expression, i.e., the expression of the changing through the existing and the available. Everywhere, universally, and every moment there functions and is realized the single principle of self-development, which conditions the motion of all the forms. The impelling causes, "mechanisms", forces, and motives of any change, motion, development should be considered as manifestations of different intensity of the contentful-formal transformations of the components in the relation {content/form} under the control of the universal principle of self-development. In the opinion of F. Engels, "in any field of natural science, even in mechanics, one makes a step forward every time when somewhere one gets rid of the word force" [20, p. 130]. Every change-motion-development is explained by the presence of two unequal, non-identical principles in their single naturally-conditioned relation, endowed, on the one hand, with an inexhaustible natural striving for unification and mutual expressiveness and, on the other hand, with no less natural incompatibility and opposition. As has already been noted earlier, it is precisely such a dual-unity and confrontation of the two principles in their natural striving for identity, which they never attain, that determines the diversity and uniqueness of the forms of the surrounding world. The relation {content/form} in combination with the formulation of the principle of self-development gives a sufficiently exhaustive representation about the directedness of all the processes, both on the gnoseological and on the ontological levels of reality, and therefore can serve as a naturallyobjective foundation for the unification of the sciences that have taken shape over the period of the gnoseological activity of human thinking.

One can speak about some connection between the components in the relations, such, say, as {content/form}, {subjective/objective}, {abstract/concrete}, {man/nature}, etc., only when the presence of one component in the other is established, when these components are mutually penetrated and expressed through each other, when some correlation is established between them. The formulation of the principle of selfdevelopment in the most compact form simply "manifests" and "fixes" such a representation about the dynamic interconnection of the componentsconstituents of reality: the development of anything whatsoever is directed at the absorption, assimilation, and reproduction of itself developing in such a way, as well as at the establishment every time of a new correlation between the investigated components "content" and "form" in their investigated relation {content/form}. As a particular case of development, as its separate branch, human cognition is also oriented at the comprehension of itself cognizing and thus developing. The objective process of the increase of the contentfulness of the forms on the gnoseological level (as a result of the active "work" of the principle of self-development on the level of thinking) is perceived and evaluated by thinking itself as the increase of the degree of its (of thinking) adequacy, which is equivalent to the perfection of the forms and ways of its representation and existence on this level (in the form) of notions, categories, theories, speculative models, etc. On the other levels the results of the "work" of the same principle are, correspondingly, different. So, say, on the level of the living inanimate (the vegetable and animal world, including man as an animal) the process of the accumulation of contentfulness is clothed in the following forms: the extraction of nutritive substances from the soil and the surrounding environment (in plants); gathering, search, hunting, and the eating of prey (in animals); the taking of food (in man). The motion and enrichment of the contentfulness by means of the reproduced and changing hereditary (inherited) forms, i.e., by means of the reproduction of offspring, — is also in the row of the contentful development on the vegetable and animal levels. In other words, at the more primitive (from the standpoint of human consciousness) animal and vegetative levels, content-based accumulations are realized, respectively, in more primitive forms — less encompassing and less contentful than at the level of thought. An indicator for assessing the results of the "work" of the same principle at these levels, similar to the indicator of adequacy for the level of thought, is the effectiveness with which an animal or a plant solves the tasks of survival and ensuring its own stable development. It follows from what has been said in the two preceding paragraphs that there is no insurmountable chasm between the content-based movement at the level of the "living inanimate" and at the level of "thought." This chasm is easily overcome if we regard thought with its forms and modes of existence (in the form of concepts, categories, speculative models, theories, doctrines, teachings, etc.) not as a kind of "ideal reflection" of something material in the human brain,

but as a continuation of the realization of the same mechanism, the same algorithm of content-based movement, but in other (specific) forms and at another level — with a bias, a shift towards ever more encompassing, ever more contentful forms of developing reality, which the aforementioned conceptual forms are. Thus, the key moment in the awareness of the essence of the transition of the contentful motion from level to level is the understanding or representation about the change of the forms of the contentful motion (towards the increase of the degree of the abstraction of the forms), but without the change of the principle (mechanism or algorithm) of this motion. Neither nature nor matter is endowed with the "universal property of reflecting", but act only as the forms of the manifestation of the action of the single universal dynamic principle of self-development, conditioning the "self-winding" or spiral-cyclic motion of everything existing, which in statics is perceived by human thinking as the reflection of itself in itself or as selfreflection. It should be noted that the forecast about the emergence and appearance of even more abstracted forms, following the form of thinking, is made here already not on an empty place, but on the basis of and with the account of the principle of self-development, conditioning the spiral-cyclic contentfulformal transformations with a bias towards the increment of the contentfulness of the next new forms. It would be the "height of superficiality" not to see that by the form of man and his thinking the contentful motion (or the motion of the contentful constituent of reality) does not end and is not exhausted. How, in what new abstracted forms this motion continues after the contentful-formal "transition" or "leap" from the form of man with his consciousness — is still to be investigated and ascertained. However, with all definiteness one can say that these next prospective forms have no bearing on any eternal paradise or hell under the patronage of a "supreme deity". The Absolute is impossible, is unrealizable in principle! Every subsequent form partly contains in a concentrated ("sublated") form (and to some extent even "surpasses" by itself) the content of the preceding form, but is by no means final and allembracing. The contentful motion is inexhaustible and at its next stages appears by means of ever new and ever more contentful (and precisely therefore ever more abstracting) forms. In this connection let us allow ourselves to adduce a small exampleillustration to what has been said. On a clear sunny morning you "walk" your dog on a forest edge. The curious animal will not fail, perhaps, to thrust its paw or nose into a forest anthill, should such turn out to be nearby. As a true lover and guardian of wild nature, or perhaps for some other considerations, you will hasten to drag the dog away from the anthill by the leash. Now put yourself in the place of the ant and imagine that anxiety and horror which take possession of you every time when an incomprehensible something, by

your standards omnipotent, but alien to all your ant essence, suddenly collapses from somewhere "above" onto your measured ant life. And then one fine day the veil falls from your eyes and it is discovered that this very most horrifying and omnipotent something sits on a short leash at another "deity" — an even more "supreme" and even more "commander-inchief", which does not let the first "all-holder" and arbiter of ant destinies run wild. For every "master" of the first level there is found its own "master" of the second level, for the chain of the forms of the contentful motion is infinite. It is not excluded that the "master" of the first level in some way "interferes" in your ant life, but it is true, however, also that he is dependent not only on the "masters" of other "supreme" or more summit levels of development, but also on your ant level. And the name of this whole structure of nested (more exactly, conjugated successive) forms and of the process generating it — is the spiral-like "self-winding" content-formal infinity. Let us hope that this extremely simplified example will at least somehow help to understand the essence of the content-formal motion. If, however, even after such an illustration you do not cease to consider that the omnipotent "deity on a leash" remains for you the most "supreme commander-in-chief", to whom, as before, one must bow in the hope of his patronage and protection, then nothing will be able to prevent you from continuing to abide in peace in the captivity of your sweet dreams and delusions. [By the way, to seek the traces of the presence of a more highly organized (i.e., gone ahead in its development) level-form, proceeding from the primitive representations about the extraterrestrial "mind" and other forms of "life", is absurd and fruitless. So far, with confidence one can assert only that this next level of the development of reality differs from the forms-levels of our cluster of reality by a substantial leap in the intensity of the contentformal transformations. To say something definite about the forms in which reality is acquired at that next level of its existence is still extremely problematic.] § 8. The problem of the objectivity and independence of the object from the subject from the position of our representations The problem of the independence-dependence of the object from the subject in the course of the establishment of the objectivity of the contentfulness in the developing forms of reality was tried to be solved and is being solved by many researchers. Here the following variant of its solution is proposed, or, more exactly, merely the next approximation to its "exhaustive" (i.e., "full and final") solution. The acute moment with which all philosophical currents in one way or another collide is the necessity of the acceptance of one or the other side in the reasonings about the independence or, on the contrary, the

interdependence of the object and the subject. Diamat, as is known, solves this task in favor of the visible and thinkable independence of the object from the subject. From our position, the question about the independence of the object from the subject or of the objective from the subjective in their corresponding relations {subject/object} and {subjective/objective} is akin to the question about the attempt of the separation and isolation of the contentful and formal components in the initial, most general (abstract) relation {contentfulness/form}. Applied to this relation, the sources of the distinction of the indicated components go back into the latent (hidden) syncretic unity. The justice of such an assessment and such a conclusion is confirmed by the point of view of G.W.F. Hegel: "In the consideration of the opposition between form and content it is essentially important not to lose sight of the fact that the content is not formless, the form at one and the same time is both contained in the content itself and represents something external to it"; and even more definitely: "The form is the content" [3, p. 298]. Supposing that the relations {subject/object} and {subjective/objective} are merely more concrete manifestations of the abstract relation {content/form}, that high rank of subjectivity [5] with which the "numerators" of the first two above-indicated relations are endowed, in our opinion, in no way excludes the deep inseparability and dependence of them on the corresponding "denominators", — except that the noted inseparability and dependence become even more latent and implicit when compared with the unambiguously manifesting interdependence of the components in the relation {content/form}. The latter circumstance (the increasing hiddenness) and serves as the source of the assertions about the independence of the components of the objective and the subjective (of the object and the subject) from each other, categorically and endlessly reproduced (the speech goes about the assertions) by the adherents of this opinion. Proceeding from our position, it is necessary to state that, exactly the opposite, the visible isolation, autonomy, self-sufficiency, and independence of the components of the objective and the subjective from each other are only relative, since subjectivity (as an active acting principle, as selfsufficiency, as selfhood, as individuality, as competence [15]) is characteristic of the contentful components (i.e., of the "numerators") of all the concrete relations of this type, and this subjectivity or greater activity of one component with respect to the other by no means abolishes their interdependence and deep inseparability, and, consequently, the feedback connection or the influence on each other. A known example of the demonstration of the presence of that same hidden syncretic inseparability of the components in the relations of this type serves also the ancient paradox concerning the establishment of the primacy of the hen or the egg.

By the way, the activity and the leading principle of the component of content in comparison with the passivity and conservatism of the component of form in the very relation {content/form}, as the forerunner of the clearly manifesting subjectivity of the "numerators" of the derivative relations {subject/object} and {subjective/objective}, likewise is not an obstacle for the deep syncretism of the components-constituents of this abstract relation. Summing up what has been said, let us muster the courage to declare that, applied to all the above-considered relations, such a question about the establishment of the primacy and the assertion of the complete independence of the components of these relations from each other is not solvable and is meaningless in principle, since in the very content or contentful component of the initial (for all the concrete relations) relation {content/form} there is already initially present the subjectivity of the content with respect to the form, as in the component with a clearly expressed sign of the active leading principle in comparison with the more passive conservative component of the form, and nevertheless the sources of the qualitative difference of one component of this relation from the other (the sources of the distinction of the components of this relation) go back into the inseparable syncretic unity. In the inanimate (thing) nature this, so to say, "subjectivity of the thing" manifests itself episodically and therefore in an explicit way is expressed rather weakly, indistinctly, veiledly, but meanwhile even there already serves as the underlay of all sorts of interactions, manifesting themselves in the form of collisions, impacts, destructions, explosions and, in the general case, of all sorts of changes (winds, hurricanes, fires, downpours, floods, earthquakes...). As the intensity of content-form transitions intensifies within a series and through a series of highly specific relations, the indicated subjectness of the content-based component "fortifies" and increasingly confidently and distinctly "steps" outward, thereby revealing the obvious confrontation and antagonism of the two constituents, the two components of a certain unified relation, until, finally, it takes shape as an allegedly separate, autonomous, and content-based subject (man or, in some cases, a higher animal) existing independently of the form (i.e., the object, nature), which has in fact lost only the clearly (visually) expressed connection with the object (nature). The notorious active principle, latently present in any element of reality (manifesting itself, for example, through the movement of electrons in atoms and molecules, or in the form of the so-called forces of gravity between celestial objects, or through streams of solar (cosmic) rays and the influence of electromagnetic fields, or in the form of earthly tornadoes, cyclones, volcanoes, etc.) — is the primordial prototype of that active content-based (subject) component of all inanimate and living matter, which subsequently, after passing through numerous intermediate forms, transforming and modifying beyond recognition, finally appears before us in the image or guise of the human

personality as a creator, a maker, a subject, while, however, being at the same time latently bound to the form-based passive (object) component in a single and integral {subject/object} relation. Thus, the mutual independence of form and content, as well as of object and subject in the cases examined, is highly relative — greater in some aspects, lesser in others; however, in any case, the reverse side of this apparent independence is their hidden, deep, essential unity and interdependence. The subjective has grown out of the so-called objective, is its continuation, but only by other forms, and already for this very reason alone they (the objective and the subjective) cannot be considered completely independent from each other. The subjective includes in itself the developed content of the objective, — in some sense the initial (source) content of the object "refracted" by the subject. The subject — this is precisely the new form of the "ascending" essence of the object. But if the subjective contains in itself the sources and the developed part of the objective, does this not mean that same latent unity with the objective after the manner of the syncretism of the constituents in the relation {content/form}? And the fact that in the guise of the feedback connection the subject is capable of exerting an influence on the object also proves their single nature and basis. Thereby the independence of the object from the subject can be recognized as purely nominal, relative, and by no means absolute. The historically accomplished and visually ascertainable separation or isolation of the subject from the object testifies only to the fact that by some part of itself the object is still contained and present in the subject and, consequently, represents also a part of their common system and therefore is dependent on it, — which, in essence, is confirmed by the possibility of the reverse influence on the object from the side of the subject. Under the objective content of reality in its ultimate expression (like the notion of the absolute truth as the motion from the relative truth in the direction of the achievement of the absolute truth) is understood the motion-development of its (of reality) contentfulness by means of the forms of different levels in the direction of the acquisition of a single generalized form of the highest (maximally possible) contentfulness. In the course of such a motion-development, registered and fixed by the human organs of sense and thinking, there takes place the change of the forms of the representation and acquisition of the content on different levels: from the inanimate, further to the living-inanimate and, then, to the living-thinkinganimate, differing by different correlations of the components of {subjective/objective} in their multilevel relations-forms. At that, each of the forms successively grows out of the preceding one, being, as it were, its continuation and remaining in the bundle (with the indispensable presence of the direct and feedback connection) with all the preceding forms-levels involved in the general spiral-cyclic content-formal motion.

Being most closely interwoven in one bundle, these forms of the motiondevelopment of the objective content nevertheless (and precisely by virtue of the mutual connectedness) are permeated and mediated by the subjective form of the conditionally finite and highest (of those reliably revealed at the given moment) level, which is the form of human thinking (consciousness). This kind of mediation cannot but tell on the assessment of the objectivity itself (of the objective content) through the prism of subjectivity, i.e., conditions the impossibility of the separation and isolation of the properly objective content from the "admixture" of the subjective content, from the presence of the component of the subjective and the impossibility of the conduct of the assessment of the objectivity outside the presence of the constituent of subjectivity. By virtue of the inevitability of the conduct of the assessment of the objectivity through the prism of subjectivity, the objective itself is penetrated by the subjective and is clothed in the form of the generally accepted and universally significant, which, however, does not prevent this form from being the continuation of the objective and at the same time being this same objective. The separate subjects or individuals are precisely for that reason similar to each other, for that very reason are like each other (both externally and internally), that in fact they represent the generalized (aggregate) form of the acquisition of the single objective content of reality, represented by uniform fragments at a quite definite level of its (of reality) motion-development. However, the set of subjective contents realized through the aggregate or row of similar to each other subjects (individuals) acquires the status of the objective content not at once and not always. The subjective contents, represented by the abstract subjectively ideal forms, can be considered objective only then, when their objectivity is verified and confirmed by the succession of the contents of all the layers-forms of the generalized pyramid (see § 6-7) and, in the first place, by the possibility and admissibility of the expression ("substitution") of the superstructural contents of the summit forms of the pyramid by the contents of the primary material-thing forms lying in the foundation of that same pyramid. Outside such a verification, the contentfulness of the previously accepted universally significant (with a "touch" of the subjective) may turn out to be an ordinary lie, approved and affirmed by the collective majority in the expectation of its momentary gains and preferences. About the ways and methods of the establishment of the objectivity of the content of the subjective forms we will speak below. Thus, the objective content — this is also, by and large, always only a relation of the type {subjective/objective}; in a pure form there is neither the one nor the other. About what form of the object can one speak outside its mediation by the subject? Outside the relations with the subject — this is merely an indeterminacy. The replenishing aggregate of the separate subjective perceptions of one and the same something and demonstrates by itself the approximation to the ever more complete expression of the

objective content of this something. The criterion of the completeness of the expression or comprehension of the objective in such a case can be only the volume (or the "quantity") of the objective contentfulness, which gives the "go-ahead" for the becoming and the success of the existence of this or that form. Let us emphasize here once again that the subjective image of the object is not the reflection of the object, but the continuation in a specific form of its (of the object) developing content (like the continuing "ascent" of the essence of the object in other, different from the thing, forms). So that complete independence of the object from the subject, as well as of the subject from the object, does not happen, — precisely by virtue of the syncretism of the connection of the contentful and formal components in the relations {subject/object}, {subjective/objective}. The simplest and most illustrative example from everyday life in confirmation of what has been said — is the relation {child/mother}. The "numerator" and "denominator" of the indicated relation correlate with each other as the components subject-object, change-basis, content-form in the corresponding relations {subject/object}, {change/basis}, {content/form}. With respect to its child, the mother, undoubtedly, acts in this case both as the basis, and as the form, and as the object — whoever, whatever might say on this account. However far from each other in space and in time the mother and her child might be, the mutual connection and interdependence are preserved forever — in the genes, in the tissues, in the thoughts, habits, behavior, way of life, preferences, etc., etc. Even by the modern, not so perfect, means of objective control to establish this kinship (or the existence of the common ineradicable objective unifying basis) presents no difficulties whatsoever. § 9. The point of view of diamat on the role of abstractions and subjectively abstract forms in the course of cognition. The interpretation of the term "interpretation" In order to advance further in the exposition of our views and approach, one will have to stop for a while on the representations of dialectical materialism about the role of abstractions and the necessity of the recourse in the course of cognition to subjectively abstract forms. The method of abstractions (or of abstracting) by the definition of the "Philosophical Encyclopedic Dictionary" [19, p. 7] — is a universal method of cognition, the logical foundation of the understanding of nature and social life, — both in the subjective sense, since by means of abstraction "we turn every thing into a logical category" [14, p. 131], and in the objective sense, since abstractions (categories of thinking) are the "expression of the regularity both of nature and of man" [12, p. 83].

The significance and depth of the elaboration of this method is testified by the following its characteristic: "Abstractions applied to the immediate sense data are called abstractions of the first order; the abstraction from the abstraction of the first order gives the abstraction of the second order, etc. Cognitive significance can be possessed by abstractions of any order, but special trust is deserved by abstractions whose results can be comprehended on material models. Such abstractions are called real, the rest — ideal (idealizations). The most important task of scientific methodology is the comprehension of the abstractions of a high order through the abstractions of a lower order or the replacement of the ideal abstractions by real ones. ...The most developed system of abstractions is possessed by mathematics, which itself in essence is the science about abstractions: they constitute its premise, method, and subject" [19, p. 7]. Here is also adduced one more extremely important, in our opinion, judgment concerning the fact that the ordinary comprehension, say, of abstract theories goes, as a rule, by way of interpretation. The need for the analysis and systematization of the abstract constructions elaborated by thinking, as well as the above-listed diversity and hierarchy of the latter, show how important it is for man (the cognizing subject, the researcher) to be able to correctly assess the truthfulness of these constructions and to have at his disposal reliable criteria and ways of such an assessment. As we see, diamat approaches the analysis of the purpose and role of abstractions quite seriously. What main thought is traced in the description of the method of abstractions? That one, that the understanding and interpretation of abstractions requires the expression of them through each other (the higher through the lower, and further both of those through the most thing-like and concrete — material ones). And the second important circumstance, noted here as well, points to the fact that the comprehension of abstractions goes, as a rule, by way of interpretation. In the indicated connection it is necessary also, in passing, to ascertain what contentfulness diamat puts into the notion of interpretation. In the broad sense interpretation means "explication, explanation, translation into a more comprehensible language" [17, p. 502]. In science and logic this notion is treated as "the aggregate of the values (meanings) attached in some way to the elements of a certain theory (to expressions, formulas, and separate symbols)". And further: "The interpretation of the theoretical constructions of the developed fields of scientific knowledge bears, as a rule, a mediated character and includes in itself multistage [compare: multilevel, — Author's note] hierarchical systems of intermediate interpretations. The connection of the initial and final links of such hierarchies is ensured by the fact that the interpretation of the interpretations of a certain theory gives its immediate interpretation. Thus, interpretation is an instrument of scientific modeling. ...In the contentful natural-scientific and mathematical theories there is always implied a certain interpretation: such theories use only the

meaningful expressions, i.e., the meaning of each expression is presupposed from the very beginning to be known" [19, p. 215]. In continuation of the list of the adduced quotations one cannot but agree also with the following interpretation: "Contentful knowledge — this is such knowledge whose terms and statements have some value and meaning, i.e., a definite interpretation. To be contentful and means nothing other than to have an interpretation" [10]. And, finally, completing the quotation of the treatments of the term "interpretation", let us adduce one more judgment, according to which the interpreting of notions means nothing other than the revelation in them of the presence of objective content [7]. § 10. The role of abstractions and subjectively abstract forms from the position of our representations In view of the importance of the above-indicated questions let us speak thoroughly about what the subjective abstract forms represent, what is their role and what are, in the end, the criteria of the truthfulness of these forms — now already from the position of our representations. As the row of the above-adduced (see § 9) interpretations and definitions shows, the process of cognition is not reduced to the necessity of the expression of the abstract forms exclusively or only through the thing forms, but solves the task of the successive revelation and confirmation of the objectivity of the contentfulnesses of all the forms present in the cognitive chain (from the thing forms, through the forms of sensations to the forms of thinking and within the framework of these latter — of the whole ramified hierarchy of abstract constructions and structures) by way of the expression of them through each other. In the final result this device or way of the mutual expression of the forms through each other and should lead to the establishment of the truthfulness or falsity of the "summit" subjective abstract forms. Such a device or way in its broadest sense is called interpreting. In the most general case the main proof of the truthfulness of the hierarchical chain of the abstract forms turns out to be their interpretability (i.e., the possibility and admissibility of the interpreting of the abstract forms by more concrete forms). As one can already guess, the particular case of the technology of interpreting, known and applied since time immemorial, is the procedure of practice, reducing to the interpreting of the abstract forms by the forms in the overwhelming majority thing-like, mediated by the forms of sensations (or by the so-called empirical experience). What, in the end, is the speech about here? In essence, about the intentions to have a substantiated possibility to express one through the other: the incomprehensible through the comprehended, the particular through the

general, the abstract through the concrete — and then this concrete, in its turn already, to replace by the successive contentful abstract, in order to operate in the further investigations precisely with the verified abstract as with the next, more productive and effective means of cognition. In distinction from the contentfulness of the form-level of the sensual (the subjects of which can serve man and the animal) the content of the formlevel of the ideal (the subject of which serves man with his thinking) within the framework, say, of the process of cognition of the above-mentioned object (the stone-boulder, see § 6) constitute the following abstractions (in the order of the increase of the degree of abstracting): the dimensions of the object (length, width, height); the form of the object (cylinder, cube, sphere, etc.); the weight (mass) of the object; the color of the object (the wavelength of the reflected light and its intensity); the hardness of the material of the object (by Rockwell, Brinell, Vickers); the strength of the object (for rupture, shear, bending, impact, etc.); the radioactivity of the material, etc., etc. All this by the animals (from the ant to the dog and further — up to the anthropoid) is simply not assessed; the knowledge of the above-listed properties of the object — this is from the field of the contentful ideal, having significance only for man as an "advanced" subject of cognition. In the establishment of the truthfulness of the abstract forms (i.e., of the presence in them of objective contentfulness) it is necessary in fact to answer the question: does the found abstract construction or structure (as the product of the form of thinking) admit the replacement or expression by means of the concrete form of thingness and/or by means of the less concrete (than the thing) form of sensations, and to what degree is such a replacement possible and admissible? For example, in order to ascertain in the simplest way the truthfulness or justice of the abstract formula describing the notorious law of Boyle-Mariotte (PV = const: the volume V of gases is inversely proportional to the pressure P [11, p. 131]), it is necessary empirically or mentally to "replace" ("substitute" or "fill") this abstraction with concrete sensations; i.e., in the change of the position of the piston in the cylinder, for instance, by pressing on the piston rod by hand, to put in correspondence to every its position (which is registered in a visual way as visual sensations) the magnitude of the response force or of the back pressure of the piston on the hand (which again is registered by the organs of touch as tactile and muscular-motor sensations). If under a certain standard set of initial conditions (constant temperature and invariable mass of the gas in the cylinder), limiting the field of applicability or justice of this law, such a replacement is possible and admissible repeatedly, then this abstract product of the form of thinking can be considered true. And in this same case, as is seen, the practical actions and their results serve as the measure or way certifying the possibility of the replacement (of one or another degree of completeness) of the found abstract form (formula) by the concrete form of another, more "down-to-earth", more "low" level (i.e., by the measurement of the displacements of the piston and of the efforts

corresponding to them). Moreover, for the increase of the mentioned degree of the completeness of the replacement (for the purpose of the expansion of the boundaries of the applicability of the abstract form) the description of the abstract form (in this case of the formula) requires, as is customary, refinement and improvement, as a result of which the initial simple abstract form (the formula description of the law of Boyle-Mariotte) under other sets of the fixed initial conditions and parameters of the surrounding environment can take the form of the laws of Gay-Lussac, Charles, Avogadro and, in the end, under the corresponding set of initial conditions can be replaced by the universal abstract form with maximally expanded boundaries of its application. As a consequence of the produced refinements and the refinement of the initial form of the law, there appears the equation of Mendeleev — Clapeyron [22]. In other words, the essence of the practical actions or the verification by practice reduces to the establishment of the possibility and admissibility of the replacement or substitution of the content of a certain abstract form (in this case of the formula description of the law of Boyle-Mariotte) by the content of the form again more concrete (in this case by the content of the form of sensations). If the possibility of such a replacement is ensured and confirmed, then there is confirmed or certified thereby also the truthfulness of the abstract form. In the final result (let us note once again) the verification by practice in fact turns out to be the particular case or variety of the broader, generalized procedure of the establishment of the truthfulness of knowledge, which is the assessment of the possibility or admissibility of the interpreting of the contents of the abstract forms by the contents of the more concrete forms, i.e., by similar kindred practical examples and by the factual material being obtained (obtained). If the abstract form admits interpreting (i.e., the filling with concrete thing content, the conjugation and succession of the contents of the compared forms with the transition of them into each other), then such an abstract form can be qualified as contentful and true. The establishment of truth includes the coordination of the contentfulnesses of the forms (the sensual and the ideal), the finding of the correspondence of the contents of the same forms and, as a perspective, the determination of the quantitative correlations between the volumes of the conjugated contents... The question of the establishment and assessment of the truthfulness of knowledge and of the presence of objective content in the abstract forms by which this knowledge is represented — is always extremely actual, and not only for the scientific and highly intellectual spheres of human activity, but also for everyday life. In the final result the most important in the process of cognition turns out to be the contentful linkage of the abstract forms with the concrete ones, admitting the interpreting or the expression of these abstract forms through

the preceding more concrete (including also the thing) forms-levels with the aim, in the future, to operate with this abstract, as with a more powerful instrument of cognition in place of the concrete forms used with the same purpose. § 11. Development as the trajectory of transformations in the course of contentful movement. The ways of the establishment of the presence and completeness of objective content Thus, the trajectory of the contentful-formal transformations in the course of the "through" contentful movement looks as follows. Taking for the conditional beginning of the contentful movement (and of the transformations of the forms) the form of thingness, we note the first transition of the content of this form (by virtue of the inevitability of the contentful-formal transformations, conditioned by the principle of selfdevelopment) into the content of the form of sensations; further we observe the transition of the content of the form of sensations (by way of the contentful-formal transformations, conditioned by the same principle of selfdevelopment) into the content of the form of thinking. Herewith the objectivity of the contents of the forms of the presented chain is assessed only in a mediated way — with the indispensable participation of the form of sensations and the form of thinking. For the establishment of the presence of objective content in the thing forms of the surrounding world serve, in the end, not only the forms of sensations (the sense organs) of man, but also the sense organs of other representatives of the animal world. Therefore not only the thing-practical activity of man or practice turns out to be the way of the assessment of the contentfulness of the thing forms; in the case of the expression of the content of the indicated thing forms by means of the content of the forms of sensations of the animal world the similar means is the behavioral reaction of the animals. The presence of objective content in the thing forms in the most simple cases is more impartially and reliably proved precisely by the comparison of the behavioral reactions (habits, grimaces, emotions, sets of sounds, etc.) of the other, besides man, representatives of the animal world. If people can agree among themselves as to the coordination of the reactions to one and the same object (which would mean the "dragging through" as objectivity of the notorious general validity of the opinions about the object as the result of a certain agreement between people), then to imagine a similar agreement between the animals in relation to the similar reactions to the investigated object is simply impossible. On the stage of the transformation of the objective content of the thing forms into the content of the forms of sensations of the representatives of the animal world, including man, there occurs the filling and saturation of the

forms of sensations with the content (contents) of the thing forms. This is the stage of the accumulation by the forms of sensations of the so-called empirical experience. The establishment of the presence of objective content in the forms of sensations and the verification (assessment) of the content of the forms of sensations for the objectivity of this content is carried out by the procedure of practice. The establishment of the objectivity of the content of the thing forms is performed by the comparison of the contents of as large as possible a number of the forms of sensations. In the coincidence or sameness of the contents of a row or a multitude of the forms of sensations there is made the conclusion about the objectivity of the content of the thing forms. If the consequences and results of the interactions of the forms of sensations with the thing forms (and of the thing forms among themselves) turn out to be approximately the same, then by such a coincidence of the contentfulnesses of the multitude of the forms of sensations of the representatives of the animal world, including man, there is proved the relatively independent of the separate forms of sensations uniformity of the content of the thing world, i.e., the objectivity of this content. Besides, by the coincidence or identity of the contents of the forms of sensations (for a row or a multitude of the representatives of the animal world, including man) there is proved not only the presence of the common objective content in the thing forms, but also the objectivity of the contents in the compared forms of sensations. Thus, at a certain stage of the movement-development of reality the content of its thing forms is transformed into the content of the more mobile and "advanced" forms, which become the forms of sensations of the representatives of the animal world, including man; in other words, the mentioned content of the thing forms "ascends", so to say, to a new level of its representation by the forms of sensations. The objectivity of the content of the thing forms, as well as of the contents of the forms of sensations, is established in a practical way (by the procedure of practice) — by the assessment of the possibility and admissibility of the replacement of the contents of some forms of sensations by the contents of other forms of sensations (i.e., by the carrying out of the indicated assessment as if "horizontally"), about which and should testify the sameness (coincidence) of the contents of these forms in their comparison. Even the very presence of the similar to each other or identical forms-carriers of sensations in the plural (for example, of human individuals) proves the succession of the contents of the form of sensations and the form of thingness and, in the final result, the objectivity of the contents of these forms. This is that which concerns the linkage of the forms of thingness and sensations. Now — about the linkage of the forms of thingness, sensations, and thinking.

The objectivity of the contents of the abstract forms of thinking is verified and confirmed by way of the comparison and the establishment of the presence in these abstract forms of the concrete content, one and common to a certain degree for the whole linkage of the forms of thingness, sensations, and thinking. The objectivity of the content of the more abstracted (in relation to the forms of thingness and sensations) forms of thinking is proved by the possibility and admissibility of the approximate expression or replacement of the content of the abstract forms by the contents of the more concrete thing forms, mediated by the forms of sensations. Besides, at the level of thinking itself, for the confirmation of the truthfulness of the purely (highly) abstract forms-products of thinking there can be attracted the less abstract formsproducts of the same thinking, already having or having received the status of genuine knowledge (the carriers of objective content) at the preceding stages of human activity. In this case the speech is about the carrying out of the assessment of the contentfulness of the forms as if "vertically". Further, to reveal the objective contentfulness of a certain abstract formproduct of thinking — this means to establish (by way of comparison) the presence in this form of the content common with the contentfulnesses of the more concrete conjugated forms, and on this basis to make the conclusion about whether the content of the abstract form "fits" into the chain of the contents of the preceding concrete forms and whether it continues by itself this chain; the positive answer to these questions means the positive answer also to the question of whether the content of the investigated abstract form can in such a way lay claim to objectivity. Science is considered a skeptical method of cognition, asserting itself over the course of 400 years in the conditions of the most severe competition of the views on this or that phenomenon (in the sense of the explanation of its foundations and the forecasting of its consequences). What, in such a case, ensured the prerogative of the scientific approach and the superiority of the way of scientific thinking (and of the obtaining of knowledge) in relation to the other methods? The installation and aim exclusively at the objectivity of the contents of the obtained knowledge, certified by the succession of the conjugated and passing into each other objective contents of the forms interpreting this knowledge. The procedure of the establishment of the presence and volume ("quantity") of objective content in this or that aggregate form, which is, say, the multilevel form of man himself, requires the assessment of the presence of the obligatory direct and reverse connection between the levels in their aggregate (aggregate form). Practice or the thing-practical activity of man precisely and acts in the role of the way or device confirming or certifying the presence of such a connection. The presence of the immediate connection between the levels testifies to their succession.

One way or another the process or procedure of the establishment of truth or of the truthfulness of the subjectively abstract forms is always carried out in a spiral-cyclic way of the return to the sources (to the foundation — to the thing forms and the forms of sensations) and thereby of the return "to oneself at a new level" (in which, in fact, is seen the essence both of the reverse connection, and of the contentful enrichment, and of the changemovement-development in the direction of the acquisition of the highest contentfulness — i.e., of objective content — by the whole aggregate of the forms). If in the course of the carrying out of such a procedure there is observed the absence or instability of the direct and reverse connections between the subjective and objective levels (the components in the relation {subject/object}), then precisely on this basis and should follow the unambiguous conclusion about the untruthfulness, incorrectness, incorrectness, inaccuracy of the verified subjective form — the product of thinking (knowledge, judgment, assertion, definition, notion, formula, theory, conception, teaching, model, etc.). In this connection it would be appropriate to add also that by virtue of the peculiarities of such a reciprocating movement (in which on any section of the path the very course or the natural spiral-like character of the development of reality presupposes the return to the foot-foundation of the pyramid, the "feeding" or enrichment of the acquired content by the primordial life-giving sources, as well as its "reverse" movement to the reforming summit, to the re-forming contentful abstractions), not only evolution, but also revolutions, and crises, and conflicts, and other upheavals are not excluded, not finished, and not completed (as this might be desired by the uninitiated and uninformed man as to the "mechanism" of the realization of the dialectical movement). § 12. The criterion of truth — contentfulness.
