Development as the movement of the forms of reality in the direction of the possession of the highest contentfulness As it was already possible to notice, the central notion on the basis of which our narration is built up and which will be even more persistently woven into the canvas of the further reasonings is the notion "content" and its derivative — "contentfulness" [soderzhatel'nost']. In accordance with the point of view reproduced above, development represents the "ascent" of objective content by the multilevel, but conjugated one with another successive forms, which in the measure of the mentioned ascent become ever more abstract and capacious, shifting or displacing into the field of the ever more contentful subject forms, i.e., in the direction of the "numerators" of the known relations {contentfulness/form}, {subject/object}, {subjective/objective}, {abstract/concrete}, {change/foundation}, etc.

Correspondingly to our approach the development of reality is carried out in the direction of the increase of the contentfulness of its forms, which, occupying new informative levels, become also ever more abstract. Let us now set ourselves the following question, actual for us: in what relation with the contentfulness of the forms is their truthfulness? According to diamat, truth — this is the objective content of the sensual, empirical experience, notions, ideas, judgments, theories, teachings, and of the integral picture of the world in the dialectics of its development [19, p. 226]. Since content is not formless, and form is not contentless (see § 8), every content, including also that answering to truth, appears in reality or is realized only through form and not otherwise, — to which, in fact, and is pointed in the very definition of truth. In other words, truth does not manifest itself in reality otherwise than through the forms (including also the forms enumerated in the definition, such as: notions, judgments, ideas, theories, teachings, empirical experience, etc.), and in the indicated aspect the question of whether truth is the form of the manifestation of objective content is a rhetorical question. By what sign (indicator, quality, property, component, constituent, etc.), proceeding from the adduced definition, should one assess the truthfulness of the forms, and including also the abstract forms of knowledge (notions, ideas, judgments, theories, teachings, etc.)? Obviously, by the presence in these forms of objective content. In other words, the contentfulness of the forms is subject to assessment, and, it seems, precisely this indicator (sign, quality, property, component, constituent, etc.) and will serve as the criterion of the assessment of the truthfulness of this or that form. Taking into account the syncretic inseparability of content and form, about which was said above, every form "from birth" is endowed with a certain contentfulness, but for human thinking the orientation serves and arouses interest, as a rule, the maximal contentfulness and, correspondingly, the form of the expression of the highest contentfulness. Therefore it is most logical to suppose that the most contentful form (i.e., the form maximally "filled" with objective content or the form possessing the highest contentfulness) and will be the true form. Thus, truth, from the position of our representations, — this is the form of the expression of objective content (i.e., the form of the expression of the highest, maximally achievable contentfulness) of reality or of its separate element at every definite stage of its (of reality) development. How, by what path, way, by what means, procedures, technologies can one assess the volume or "quantity" of the contentfulness of this or that investigated abstract form? By way of the expression or "substitution" (at least mental) of the content of the investigated abstract form by the already assessed, verified, approved, and affirmed by man (i.e., endowed with the

status of true) contents of the more concrete forms. Such a procedure is named practice, and within the framework of the broader application of such a procedure or technology — interpreting. In both cases the mentioned procedures reduce to the assessment of the possibility and admissibility of the expression or substitution of the content of the investigated abstract forms by the contents of the more concrete and already verified forms (after the manner of the above-described in § 10 way or procedure of the assessment of the truthfulness of the abstract form-formula of the law of Boyle-Mariotte). Here is one more example of the assessment of the contentfulness and truthfulness of the forms. Simply speaking, if the volume of a spherical vessel of radius R is expressed, according to the "machinations" of abstract thinking, by the formula (4/3)πR³, then it does not constitute any difficulties to verify or assess the volume calculated in such a way, pouring into the vessel water or pouring river sand. By the assessment of the contentfulness of the form of thinking (i.e., of the above-indicated formula), carried out in this case by the way or procedure of the substitution of the content of the abstract form of thinking by the content of the concrete form of sensations (filling the vessel with the then measured quantity of water or sand), one can ascertain the truthfulness of the formula, i.e., the truthfulness of the formproduct of abstract thinking. And further, this abstract form (the formula for the computation of the volume of the sphere), verified a million times for the correspondence or "coincidence" of its content with the content of the form of sensations, can, in its turn, be used for the verification and assessment of the contentfulness of the other forms, to refer to it and to use it in the further investigations as the foundation with the confirmed and certified truthfulness. Thus, that criterion (sign, indicator, quality, property, component, etc.) by which one should assess truth or in the general case the truthfulness of the arising, existing, and disappearing forms — this is by no means practice, but the contentfulness of the forms. Practice, however, — this is merely the procedure (process) of the carrying out of such an assessment, and moreover limited mainly by the thing field of the activity of man. In relation to the broader field of application, including the assessment of the truthfulness of the abstract constructions, structures, and edifices of various hierarchy and nesting, such a procedure is named interpretation (see § 9) or interpreting. In this connection practice turns out to be merely the particular case, the narrow layer of the more extensive procedure or technology of the establishment of the truthfulness of the forms — interpreting. Thereby practice — this is also interpreting, i.e., the replacement (often mental) of some forms, as a rule more abstract, by the more concrete and verified forms (in this same row is found also the process of modeling). [Practice — this is, above all, a process, and therefore in a certain sense an indeterminacy, which in itself is not very suitable for the definition of a

criterion. From the position of our reasonings the criterion of truth is not practice itself or the thing-practical activity of the subject with its results, but the presence and volume or "quantity" of objective content in the investigated abstract forms, which and is established with the help of the procedure of practice or in the general case of the technology of interpreting.] The genuine criterion of the assessment of the truthfulness of the abstract forms-products of thinking is the contentfulness of these forms. Practice, however, or interpreting — this is the technology (procedure, way, process, means, etc.) of the establishment of the presence and "quantity" of contentfulness (i.e., of the presence and "quantity" of objective content) in the investigated forms, and hence, in the final result, also of truth or of the truthfulness of the investigated forms. On the first stage of the realization of the mentioned technology there is established the presence of objective content (contentfulness) in the investigated forms by way of the assessment or verification of the possibility and admissibility of the as complete as possible substitution (expression, replacement) of the contents of the investigated forms by the contents of the more concrete forms (already verified and with the confirmed truthfulness), i.e., there is established the admissibility of the replacement of one by the other (the unknown by the known, the incomprehensible by the comprehended, the irrational by the rational, etc.). By the results of such a verification there is made the conclusion about the interpretability of the investigated forms, i.e., about the succession of the substituting and substituted contents. The interpretability of the investigated forms in such a case means the confirmation of the presence of a certain "quantity" of objective content (contentfulness) in the investigated forms. On the second stage there is assessed the completeness of such a substitution (i.e., there is determined the "quantity" of the contentfulness of the investigated forms) by way of the finding or establishment of the as large as possible number of the concrete forms (already verified and with the confirmed truthfulness) suitable for the carrying out of the above-indicated replacement. By the results of the performed assessment there is made the conclusion about the completeness of the contentfulness, and then also about the truthfulness of the investigated forms. Both stages can be united into one and realized simultaneously. Thus, the interpretability of the investigated forms and the number of interpretations give a more or less complete representation about the status (truthfulness or untruthfulness) of the investigated forms. On the basis of the above-adduced reasonings one can come to that conclusion that the genuine criterion of the truthfulness of the forms serves their contentfulness, and the status of the true form or of truth

answers to the form with the maximally achievable (at this or that stage of the development of reality) contentfulness. What, then, is the contentfulness of this or that form? One should suppose that in application to the concrete objects and other material elements of reality the contentfulness (as the predominantly internal hidden component or constituent) of the concrete forms must nevertheless be represented from outside by means of some concrete external attributes of these objects or elements. Can the manifestation of contentfulness be, for instance, the beauty of the form? Probably, it can. Beauty may well turn out to be one of the attributes suitable for the assessment of contentfulness, and consequently, also for the assessment of the truthfulness of a certain concrete form. The same thing, obviously, can be said also about such manifestations of the contentfulness of the concrete forms as simplicity (or, on the contrary, complexity), economy (economicalness), usefulness, pragmaticalness, rationality, systemicity, integrity, originality, etc. Now as to the contentfulness of the abstract forms: theories, conceptions, paradigms, plans, schemes, formulas, projects, diagrams, models, teachings, etc. Beauty (for instance, mathematical beauty and the elegance of the formula) also can turn out to be the external manifestation suitable for the assessment of the contentfulness of this or that abstract form. The external embodiment of the contentfulness of the abstract forms may well be also the clarity of the judgment, and the harmony of the exposition, and the demonstrativeness of the assertion, and the laconism of the thought (idea), and the logicality of the conclusions, and the non-contradictoriness of the theory, and the general validity of the results, and the informativeness of the diagram, and the simplicity of the scheme, and the compactness of the planning, etc., etc. However, in whatever clothes the contentfulness of the concrete and abstract forms may dress itself, in both cases the contentfulness in the person of its above-indicated manifestations must be confirmed by the interpreting of the assessed forms either by the results of the thing-practical activity (practice), or by the repeatedly verified and approved results (products) of the theoretical (mental, subjectively ideal) activity of man. The procedure of interpreting, however, presupposes under itself the as complete as possible expression, replacement, or substitution (at least mental) of the content of the investigated (verified) abstract form by the contents of the more concrete forms. Then by the interpretability of the forms (i.e., by the possibility and admissibility of the contents of the investigated forms to be expressed or substituted by the contents of the more concrete and verified forms) there is proved the presence of objective content in the investigated abstract forms. The maximal or highest contentfulness, and hence also the truthfulness of the investigated forms, in its turn, is proved by the possibility and admissibility of the expression or substitution of the content of the abstract

form by the concrete contents of the maximally possible number of the concrete forms of different levels of concreteness (by the practical examples, the factual material, the results of the numerous experiments, the repeatedly verified and affirmed by the scientific community results of the theoretical investigations, etc.). Only then the form with the contentfulness assessed in such a way can lay claim to a place in the list of the maximally contentful abstract forms, and, consequently, also of the true forms. In other words, the truthfulness, i.e., the maximal or in the extreme case the sufficient contentfulness of the abstract form, must be certified and confirmed by its interpretability (the possibility and admissibility of being expressed) by the contents of the other forms: — the more concrete, i.e., of the more "low" (by hierarchy) level of the forms; — of the abstract forms whose truthfulness is confirmed earlier and does not arouse doubts, which and would testify to the succession of the contents of all these forms of different levels, and, consequently, also to their objectivity. Here it is appropriate to return to the criterion of truthfulness proposed by A. Poincaré (see § 3). Analyzing in detail the statement of the French scientist, one can see that from his point of view the "internal harmony of the world" and is the objective contentfulness conditioning the truthfulness of this (real) world. Can the harmony of the world be the manifestation of its contentfulness? Obviously, it can, as also beauty. The matter remains only for the small — to decide how to assess this contentfulness (i.e., the internal harmony of the world or of reality), in what conventional units or standards one should express it, in order to reveal the highest degree (level) of this contentfulness, which and will answer to the true form of reality. And with all this the assessment of the contentfulness of the investigated form (the internal harmony of the world by A. Poincaré) must be conducted, as was noted above, in full correspondence with the technology of interpreting (i.e., with the establishment of the admissibility and completeness of the substitution of the content of the investigated form by the contents of the possibly larger number of the concrete forms, as well as of the abstract forms whose truthfulness is confirmed earlier and does not arouse doubts). In view of the special importance of the considered question let us reproduce once again the substantiation of the above-advanced criterion of truth (of the truthfulness of the forms of reality). Since the multitude of the dictionaries, including also the philosophical ones, interpret the criterion as the sign on the basis of which there is produced the assessment of something [16, p. 130; 17, p. 664] and by which one can determine the truthfulness or falsity of the judgment [21], as well as as the

"distinctive mark by means of which we can recognize it (truth)" [6], then with all confidence one can say that such a sign or indicator in the case of the assessment of the truthfulness of the forms (i.e., in the process of the search and establishment of truth) is by no means practice. Here, for example, is what is reported in the "Encyclopedic Dictionary" of F. A. Brockhaus and I. A. Efron [21]: "The formal criterion of truth serve the logical laws: everything that does not contain in itself an internal contradiction is logically possible, but in order to be true, it must correspond also to the material criterion, i.e., to the factual content of the thought." As we see, in the final account and here as the criterion of the truthfulness of the forms there is advanced their factual (objective) contentfulness, which by no means diverges from our representations and our position. After all, to the most correct and appropriate in this connection question — by what uniform sign or indicator one should assess the truthfulness of the abstract forms, what quality or property of the abstract form is subject to assessment for the revelation and certification of its truthfulness — the answer suggests itself the following: the different forms can be correctly compared, juxtaposed, assessed only by the sign inherent to any and every form and named contentfulness (i.e., by the completeness or filledness of the form with content). The most true, genuine, and actual is the form possessing the highest contentfulness at the given moment of its (of the form) development; the less contentful form is both less true, and less reliable, and less effective, and less actual. The filledness of the abstract form with internal content is assessed, established, verified by the external manifestations of the form, such, say, as beauty, rationality, simplicity, obviousness, laconism, etc. But how, in what manner, by what path, way, with the help of what procedure is such an assessment performed — this is already a question of technology. By practice is meant the procedure of the assessment of the truthfulness of the abstract forms, connected with the determination of the possibility and admissibility of the substitution of the content of the abstract form by the contents of the concrete forms, namely — by the contents of the thing forms, mediated by the forms of sensations. From the heights of the present day this procedure is somewhat "narrow" in order to embrace by itself the whole broadest spectrum of the fields of the activity of the modern researchers. Therefore for the assessment of the truthfulness of the forms in all the fields of the activity of modern man there is applied the procedure named interpreting. The technology of interpreting is connected with the determination of the possibility and admissibility of the as complete as possible substitution of the content of the abstract form not only by the contents of the forms of sensations and of the thing forms, mediated by the human sensations (as in the case of the procedure of practice), but also by the contents of such abstract forms-products of thinking whose truthfulness is preliminarily verified and repeatedly confirmed in the course of the preceding activity of man (the speech is about the practical examples, the empirical data, the

factual material, the formula expressions and computations, the schemes, the models, the theories, the teachings, the axioms, the postulates, etc., etc., whose truthfulness and actuality does not arouse doubt, above all, in the scientific circles or in the milieu of the learned public). In such a connection the procedure of practice turns out to be the particular case of the technology of interpreting; i.e., practice also belongs to the technology of interpreting, but with that only difference that here the procedure of the assessment of the truthfulness of the forms is carried out by the substitution (possibly mental) of the contents of the abstract forms by the contents mainly of such concrete forms as the forms of thingness, mediated by the forms of sensations. Let us emphasize, thus, once again, that not practice or in the general case the technology of interpreting serve as the criteria of the truthfulness of the abstract forms, but such an omnipresent component or constituent of all the forms as their internal contentfulness (filledness or the degree of the filledness with content). The procedure of practice or the technology of interpreting allow only to assess the presence and completeness (i.e., the "quantity") of the contentfulness (and hence, also the degree or level of the truthfulness) of the investigated forms. And now on the basis of the understanding of the essence of the criterion of the truthfulness of the forms there appears the possibility to meaningfully develop its (of the criterion) numerical standards — to build, say, scales, diagrams, nomograms, rating systems, etc. for the numerical (quantitative) assessment, firstly, of the contentfulness and, secondly, of the truthfulness of the all possible forms. The criterion of the assessment of the truthfulness of the form — this is its contentfulness, and the level of the truthfulness or genuineness of the form is determined by the completeness of the content or by the "quantity" of this very contentfulness. Hence truth — this is the form of the highest contentfulness or of the maximally achievable completeness of the content at every definite moment of the development of this form. Any form of the highest contentfulness (i.e., the form maximally filled with content) at every given moment of its (of the form) development and is the true form. In such a context it is easily understood that in its essence absolute truth — this is an iterative process of the infinite approximations to the exhaustive (final and complete) expression of the objective content of the developing reality by the diverse its (of reality) forms of the highest contentfulness. If practice (as a procedure, as a technology, as a way, as a process, etc.), erroneously acting until now in the role of the criterion of truth, does not yield to the quantitative assessment and therefore cannot serve as the informative criterion in the establishment of the truthfulness of the forms, then the contentfulness of the forms — this is such a sign or indicator which organically, in a natural way is suitable and adapted for such an assessment.

Someone, surely, will say what nonsense is being carried on here — how can one measure the contentfulness and the more so the truthfulness of something? The answer is simple: man from time immemorial is engaged only in that he assesses and measures the contentfulness of the all possible forms of reality. From now on he will do this more meaningfully. In every sphere of human activity there exist their own rating systems, scales, graphs, tables, diagrams, nomograms, etc. for the assessment of the contentfulness of the diverse forms. The metric system of measures, the systems SI, CGS, etc. with their conventional units and standards (kilograms, meters, seconds, etc.), the monetary system, the virtual system Bitcoin (or something similar crypto-currency, coming to replace and as the continuation of money), the indices IQ, Hirsch, RSCI, etc. (as the attempts of the expression in conventional units of the level of the intellect of man), the humanistic systems of the assessment of spirituality (as the attempts of the assessment in conventional units of the spiritual potential of man) — all this is the examples of the rating systems allowing to assess the contentfulness of the all possible forms of reality, abiding at its different levels and in different fields. The "quantity" of the measured contentfulness of the forms and answers to the different approximations of these forms to the absolute truth or to the form of the "exhaustive" expression of the objective content of reality (in this or that its field). Man as one of the multitude of the forms of the multilevel reality, in his turn, represents a multilevel formation. But from the ancient times and until now the contentfulness of man is determined mainly by the level of the material accumulations, present and, unfortunately, dominating (for now) among the other levels in their hierarchical aggregate. Money, herewith, and is the quantitative measure of the contentfulness of this dominant (i.e., of the dominating level in the composition of the aggregate of all the other levels of man), why they and occupy a special place and are the most widespread rating system among the other systems and scales of the quantitative assessment of the contentfulness of the forms. Being, however, the measure of the contentfulness mainly of the "down-to-earth" material levels and sublevels in the composition of the multilevel form of man, the rating monetary system by virtue of the indicated circumstance is not so universal as it seems, and is "powerless" in relation to the assessment, say, of the spiritual and other summit spheres and levels of the "abiding" of the true contentfulness of the human "I". Spirituality or morality, being the aggregate of the deep human feelings, mediated by the human thinking (consciousness), in general poorly yields to the quantitative assessment with the use of the existing "down-to-earth" standards. For the assessment of the spiritual potential of man there is required a special filigree-refined scale. Money — this is a primitive "measurer" of the volumes of the content or potential of the forms mainly of the material-thing level (and not a certain "universal equivalent"; the incorrect or outdated basic representations sometimes seriously hinder the understanding of the essence of the

problem), behind the "monumental" facade of which is hidden the unpretentious essence of this "gasket" as one more superfluous intermediate element (similar to the notions "reflection" or "copy" in the known epistemological theories), the need for which will fall away in the measure of the perfection of the organization of the social structure. In the same way as the scales, the clocks, the various kinds of counters (electric, gas, water) and other counting devices serve as the simple measurers of the volumes of the content or of the contentful resource of these or those forms of different levels (of electric energy, heat, water, gas, oil, air, time, etc.), money — this is also a "mechanical" measurer of the contentful potential of the all possible forms-levels, assessed again by the substitution of the contents both of the concrete and of the abstract forms by the content of the very concrete material form. In other words, money — this is also a trivial (with the claim to universality) rating system for the measurement of the "quantity" of the contentfulness of the forms. As is known, every movement forward consists in that a step is made from the achieved, and all the perspective forms (such, say, as the plans, the projects, the programs, etc.), before receiving the approval for their embodiment and application, are verified by way of the replacement (at least mental) by the existing (achieved) and already applied by man forms. If the contents of the investigated (verified) forms can be substituted (as completely as possible) by the contents of the already used forms (i.e., of the forms endowed with objective contents — of the true forms), then there is made the conclusion both about the sufficient contentfulness of the investigated forms, and about their suitability for the further application. "The success of human practice", consider the Marxists, proves the correspondence of the representations of man "to the objective nature of things" which he perceives [11, pp. 134-135]. The speech here is about the correspondence of the content of the products of human thinking (of these or those abstract subjectively ideal constructions and edifices) to the content of the thing world, i.e., about the truthfulness of the abstract forms produced by human thinking, which for man as the subject of cognition is the most interesting, valuable, and important in the first place. The condition of the "success" of the application, and hence, also the sign (criterion) of the truthfulness of the abstract form-product of thinking will be the correspondence of its content to the content of the form of thingness, or, in other words, the possibility and admissibility of the as complete as possible substitution of the content of the abstract form of thinking by the content of the thing forms, mediated by the forms of sensations. In the final result the presence of objective content in the investigated abstract form — here is the criterion of the truthfulness of this form, and hence, also of the "success" of its application in practice. As we see, neither "human practice" nor "success" are the key words in the above-adduced assertion; the determining factor of the success of the practical application or of the truthfulness of this or that abstract form turns out to be its objective contentfulness.

Thus, as to the recognition of the special role and significance of practice in the assessment process of the establishment of truth there are no objections with that only difference, however, that practice in this case is not the criterion of the truthfulness or of the success of the application of the investigated abstract constructions, but serves only as the device or way of such an assessment. The genuine criterion of truth or of the success of the application of the abstract forms turns out to be the contentfulness of the verified forms, and more precisely — the "quantity" of this contentfulness, on the basis of which it becomes possible, finally, to consciously assess the truthfulness of the investigated forms quantitatively, i.e., by number or by "digit". If the sense of practice reduces to the assessment of the possibility and admissibility of the substitution of the content of the abstract forms by the contents of the forms mainly thing (and more precisely, by the contents of the forms of sensations), then the technology of interpreting is represented by a more expanded set of the means and devices of the establishment and verification of the truthfulness of the abstract forms: and by the forms of sensations, and by the already verified, approved, and recognized (affirmed) in their truthfulness abstract forms of different levels and constructions. The multilevel abstractions with the established and confirmed truthfulness — this is also the steps of the "ascent" of the essence or of the objective content of reality. In the former times the appeal to the procedure of practice was sufficient; today this procedure must be expanded by way of the conscious inclusion in its assessment arsenal of the set of the modern and more effective means — otherwise man, relying on the false or unskillfully and erroneously chosen orientations ("untrue" truths), risks losing the adequacy of the perception of the world and disappearing as a species or a certain form-level of reality. The well-known expression (the prototypes of which with great probability are found in the works of G. W. F. Hegel): "Truth is always concrete", — is completely correct from that point of view that truth at every given moment — this is merely the concrete limited form of the next approximation to the all-embracing expression of the moving, developing content (as the equivalent of the "extracted" from the depth of the abstract essence) of reality. Besides, the truthfulness of the contents (i.e., the completeness or the "quantity" of the contentfulness) of the investigated abstract formsproducts of thinking (notions, judgments, projects, theories, conceptions, philosophical systems, teachings, etc.) is established by the admissibility of the interpreting and by the number of the interpretations of these investigated forms by the known point finite examples: by the factual material, by the results of the thing-practical and theoretical activity, as well as by the already verified and confirmed abstract forms. In such a section it is easily understood that all the perspective forms laying claim to the

acquisition of the status of the true forms also subsequently will turn out to be the concrete relative truths by virtue of the interpreting of them by the concrete finite examples-results of the preceding practical and theoretical activity in the process of the establishment of their truthfulness. The "success" of the existence (i.e., the truthfulness, actuality, viability) of any real form is determined by its contentful resource or again by the "quantity" of its contentfulness. The contentful resource, in its turn, is made up of the multitude of the constituents; for such a form as, for example, the state — this is by no means only the developed economy, but also the intellectual-spiritual potential of the population, the reserves of the natural resources and the defense potential, the self-sufficiency, the high adaptability of the population to the changing conditions with the preservation of the adequacy of thinking, etc. In application, say, to the living forms — this is and the endurance of the organism, and its survivability, and its energy-arming or energy potential, and the duration of life (without the loss of quality), and the adequate reaction to the influences and the ability to adapt to the changing conditions of the external environment, and again the self-sufficiency, etc. Good-bad, better-worse, higher-lower, wide-narrow, much-little, more-less, deep-shallow, useful-harmful — the origin and the wide use of these and of the multitude of the similar pairs of comparisons is also conditioned by the need in the assessment of the completeness of the contentfulness or of the contentful resource of the all possible forms of reality mainly at the everyday level. Analogously the success of the actions or the successfulness of the activity of man is determined by the contentful resource of the form of man (in the general case — of mankind) and of his thinking. Any form-level lives or exists, enriching itself in a natural way, until such time as any of the subforms-sublevels entering into the composition of the whole aggregate of the subforms-sublevels of the form does not express by itself the maximally achievable for this subform-sublevel contentfulness. With the onset of the moment of the expressedness of the maximal contentfulness by some subform-sublevel in the composition of the existing form (which answers to the achievement of the peak of the activity of the whole form) there begins to prevail the progressive taking away of its contentfulness by the neighboring forms-levels. For this reason the form stagnates and regresses, being "dragged apart" into parts and assimilated by the other more active forms-levels by virtue of the most severe competition between them and of the inexhaustible "demand" for contentfulness. Every "gaping" form, having stopped in the increase of its content (contentful potential) and having slowed down the development, the competitors immediately drag apart into the constituents, replenishing thereby the reserves of their contentfulness correspondingly to the omnipresent principle of self-

development. The boundaries, the resource, the quality of every form are conditioned by the volume of the contentfulness accumulated in it. All the forms-levels have to prove their viability (i.e., the ability to increase and multiply their potential-contentfulness) in the hard competitive struggle. Man — is not an exception, but a specimen of the typical form submitting to the action of the universal "mechanism" of the development of reality — the principle of self-development. The very existence of any form takes place only thanks to the increase and preservation of the rates of the growth of its contentfulness, and the very fact of its existence testifies to the presence in it of the sufficient volume of contentfulness. On the other hand, the highest contentfulness of this or that level is demonstrated, confirmed, and certified by the stability of its form. Thus, the "success" or the duration of the stable existence of the form is the consequence and the indicator of the presence in this form of the sufficient volume of contentfulness, and this subject-to-assessment contentfulness (of the ever-increasing degree of abstractness) of the forms must be tied up with the concreteness of the preceding forms, i.e., admit the interpreting by the preceding more concrete forms-levels. What advantages promises such, it would seem, insignificant refinement concerning the advancement and substantiation of the correct and genuine criterion of truth? In the "guise" of the newly advanced criterion the researcher receives at his disposal an effective instrument for the carrying out of the conscious comparative quantitative assessment of the contentfulness (and hence, also of the truthfulness) of the diverse forms and, correspondingly, of the building of them by rank. And already on this basis there opens the possibility to take the more competent and weighed decisions concerning the determination of the direction of the further investigations of reality with the reliance on the meaningfully selected true its forms. § 13. The technology of interpretation as a universal way of the assessment of the contentfulness of forms Let us examine in greater detail the technology of interpretation applied in the assessment and establishment of the contentfulness and truthfulness of the investigated forms. As it has already been clarified above (see § 9), "to interpret" — means to express something abstract and insufficiently definite through the thorough, definite, and concrete; to translate the incomprehensible into the language of the comprehensible; to present the new and unusual by means of the known and habitual. In an analogous way to interpret a notion or to give a definition — this means to express the particular through the more general, or, in the

words of V. I. Lenin, "this means, first of all, to bring the given notion under another, broader one" [11, p. 142]. In the indicated cases one thing is expressed through another: something that is only just taking shape, unclear and vague — through the established and definite preceding one. In this sense the technology of interpretation fits into the channel of the principle of self-development (and submits to this principle), which determines and prescribes the unitary directionality of development to all real processes. In other words, interpretation — this is both the elucidation (explication) of abstract constructions by way of the recourse to the content of concrete forms; and the expression of the irrational through the rational; and the assignment of concrete sense-values to abstract elements: images, notions, judgments, expressions, computations, schemes, formulas, symbols, etc. All the scientific devices of the establishment of truth, including the explanation and forecasting of the results of investigations (among which there turn out to be: the modeling of processes, the reproduction of real situations, the simulation of behavior, the anticipation of consequences, etc.), relate one way or another to the technology (method, way, procedure) of interpretation. The most marketable and widespread way of the confirmation of the contentfulness of abstract forms — this is the interpretation of these forms by the habitual and accepted (accepted by reason, by the everyday consciousness, or by the so-called common sense) particularities and concretions. The recourse to facts (examples, empirical data, approved results of theoretical activity) with the aim of the expression of the contents of the investigated abstract forms by the contents of more concrete forms — there is the interpretation of the abstract by the concrete (such a practice is applied in relation to all subjectively ideal forms from different spheres of human activity, and not only to the products and results of theoretical investigations). The ability or suitability of an abstract form to interpretation by other, more concrete forms (i.e., the interpretability of the abstract form) by no means yet speaks of and does not testify to the sufficient contentfulness of this form, but nevertheless — this is the first obligatory condition on the path of the certification of the truthfulness of this or that form. The second obligatory condition in the establishment of the truthfulness of a form — the determination of the completeness of its contentfulness and, consequently, of the level of its truthfulness depending on the "quantity" of this contentfulness, conditioned by the number (and as large as possible) of the interpretations of the content of the investigated form by the contents of more concrete forms.

By the admission and conclusion of the Marxists themselves, the criterion of truth adopted by them in the theory of cognition, built on the basis of practice, is to a certain extent indefinite, i.e., is not exhaustive (necessary and sufficient). But the point here is by no means in the perfectionimperfection of the procedure (practice) as a criterion; the indefiniteness of any of the possible criteria of truth in any case is conditioned by the fact that, firstly, none of the investigated (tested for truthfulness) real forms can be contentfully exhaustive or absolutely contentful and, secondly, the highest contentfulness of none of the forms can be confirmed by an infinite number of interpretations. Here the principle of reasonable sufficiency comes into force, on which one should orient oneself in practice in the establishment of truth with the use of the technology of interpretation. The same thing relates to the process of cognition in the first place. In the order of the decrease of the degree of concreteness and the increase of the abstractness of forms the trust in the method of interpretation as a procedure or technology of the assessment of the contentfulness of forms is distributed in the following way: first the preference is given to the substituting forms of "empirical experience", then to the practical examples and factual material and further — to the already in advance verified, accepted, and approved in the quality of true abstract (theoretical) constructions and structures. Why does the factual, concrete material of the thing world (i.e., the form of thingness) dominate in relation to the superstructural, abstract subjectively ideal forms? Firstly, this level of the development of forms is historically primary; the thing forms and the forms of sensations belong to the sphere of the so-called everyday "empirical experience" of man. After all, we walk and run on the earth by no means only and not so much thanks to the mind or intellect, not owing to the presence of abstract thinking and its potential readiness to the construction and comprehension of abstract schemes and models, but in the first place, by virtue of the presence in the general linkage or aggregate of the forms of the "human" nature of the earlier in time of their origin foundation-forming forms-levels — precisely of those forms which are defined as "living", but so far without the accompanying prefixes: thinking, animate, highly organized, intellectual, spiritual, etc. Secondly, in the process of the cognitive activity of man everything unknown and unexplored is always expressed through the already known and comprehended. And since the general process of development (as well as its specific form of a quite definite level — cognition) represents the infinite movement of the contentful constituent through the ever more and more contentful forms-levels, characterized by their uniqueness, unrepeatability, and irreversibility, — it is easily realized that the only possible way (procedure, algorithm, or technology) of the realization of such a movementdevelopment, as well as of cognition, — this is "the making of oneself from oneself while changing" with the reliance on the achieved and with the indispensable use of the contents of the forms of the preceding levels.

Hence, by virtue of the striving to the ever more contentfully capacious abstract forms there naturally takes shape also the hierarchy of the formslevels, manifesting itself through their branching aggregate (from the thing forms to the forms of thinking). And all this diversity is determined by the unidirectionality of the movement-development (see the formulation of the principle of self-development, § 7). In this and consists the sense of the movement in the direction of the irreversible "height": the new content grows out of the available, out of the accumulated, out of the already achieved by way of "the making of oneself from oneself" and simultaneously turns out to be different from itself. [Even a simple external comparison shows that the technology of interpretation as an unalterable element of the cognition of the changing reality turns out to be the embodiment of the same universal principle of self-development]. And again let us turn to the verified system of views of dialectical materialism. "The practical activity of man billions of times had to bring the consciousness of man to the repetition of different logical figures, so that these figures could acquire the significance of axioms" [12, p. 172], — these well-known words of V. I. Lenin give an unambiguous representation of how long a path (in the course of everyday observations and routine actions, contributing to the accumulation of "empirical experience") the true abstract logical constructions, filled with concrete contentful sense (the speech is about the initial forms of sensations and thinking), take shape. Becoming ever more contentful, the mentioned logical figures with time themselves can serve as the foundations or axiomatic basis, as well as the landmarks and indicators of the directionality of the subsequent activity of man, both practical and theoretical. In such an uncomplicated trivial way the multilevel reality is built. Here I would like to note without fail that the moment of the latent contentful form-formation is disclosed by our greatest native dialectician V. I. Lenin so masterfully and conveyed by such a filigree-precise formulation that nothing can be taken away from it or added to it, however much one would like to. Thus, the ever more abstracting itself in the course of the development of reality contentfulness of its forms indicates at the same time also the perspective direction of the variability of the forms of the representation of this reality. The maximally achievable (highest) contentfulness — here is the guiding star for all the changing forms on all possible levels of development. The different logical figures, about which V. I. Lenin speaks, are nothing else but the labor skills, habits, customs, traditions, rites, principles, convictions, ideals, preferences, cultural codes, values, rules, and laws of the behavior of people, taking shape in different human communities over the course of millennia. These are those abstract constructions and structures which, being filled with contentful sense, lead through life from birth to death both the separate individual and the numerous their groupings, not letting them stray

from the path — from the road, in its essence, righteous and true, with the course on the acquisition of the highest contentfulness by the human forms following this road. Since the sensuous in the multilevel human nature takes the upper hand over the "supersensuous" (mental), the level of thinking (consciousness) tries in a natural and most obvious, primitive way (for the most part, by the "mechanical" path) to rationalize everything contentful (especially of the abstract kind), i.e., to express it through quite concrete forms of sensations, which turn out to be for the human consciousness closer, "dearer", and more comprehensible (more thorough) than all the other forms. The technology of interpretation — this is the universal way of the assessment of the contentfulness of all possible forms of reality, but since in our cluster of reality the first-born foundation-forming forms are the thing, material, substantial forms, then in the calculation, in the first place, are taken and the emphasis for the most part is placed on these fundamental "down-to-earth" levels-forms. In order to establish the truthfulness, reliability, authenticity, actuality of this or that abstract form, it is necessary to reveal and "extract" from the compared abstract and concrete forms the common ("as the one in the many or the likeness of the many" [18, p. 351]) for them content. The confirmation of the contentfulness of a theory, conception, or any other abstract form is ensured by the realization of the procedure of the comparison of the contents of different concrete forms with the content of the abstract form with the aim of the revelation of the common or coinciding "shares" of the contents of the compared forms and the establishment thereby of the possibility of the replacement (substitution) of the content of the abstract form by the contents of the concrete forms. In the quality of the common content of all the compared forms there may turn out to be some single universal principle, "mechanism", postulate, or algorithm, common for all the forms engaged in the indicated procedure. In the approximate coincidence of the contents of the forms of sensations and the abstract forms of thinking there is made the conclusion about the contentfulness precisely of the abstract form (since the most interesting for man is the moment of the as large as possible coincidability and succession of the contents of the abstract and thing-sensuous forms). In the opposite case (without the carrying out of the verification of the possibility of the replacement of the contents of the indicated forms), giving free rein to one's subjective abstract fantasies, one can fantasize god knows what. Therefore from time immemorial man is engaged in the search of the criteria for the determination of the truthfulness, in the first place, of the subjective abstract constructions and structures. The inadequate perception of the world, conditioned by the abstract inventions, false premises, and conclusions, can bring and brings, in the final

result, harm to man and mankind. For this reason man must pay heightened attention to the assessment and verification for contentfulness and reliability of all without exception abstract forms produced in his head, of all human suppositions, hypotheses, inventions, creative ideas, and fantasies (including and especially the publications in the mass media, see § 17). The inaccuracy and negligence in this respect, the excessive credulity to the unverified fabrications brings to man, as well as to the whole of mankind as a whole, immeasurably more harm than the benefit from the momentary gain built on the foundation of false representations and conclusions. The untruthfulness of the abstract forms of thinking means their low-contentfulness (i.e., the insufficient filling of these forms with objective content), about which and should testify the non-succession and non-conjugacy of them with the preceding concrete forms of sensations and thingness. Thus, the abstract forms as the products of human thinking — these are the special, specific forms of the representation, the same thing, of the objective content of reality at the next definite stage of the movement-development of this very reality. The establishment of the true knowledge about the object is equivalent to the procedure of the mental revelation and "extraction" of the objective content from the thing form and the correlation of it with the transformed contents of the more abstracted forms of other levels, namely: the form of sensations and the form of thinking. It goes without saying that after the transformation into other forms and representations in other forms of other levels, this thing objective content does not remain identical to itself or equal to the content of the formscarriers of thingness, — otherwise it would not change its form. The changes of the contents accompanying such transformations of the mentioned chain of forms and represent by themselves the peculiarities or the "highlights" of those forms into which the objective content is transformed in the measure of its movement-development or "ascent" from the thing forms. Every subsequent form of the indicated chain includes in itself in the "folded", compressed form the contents of the preceding forms and something else special, different from them — a certain "weight" of novelty and unrepeatability. The thing form — this is the source and forerunner of the human sensations. However, to compare the content of this form with the content of the form of thinking does not present itself possible, since the content of the form of thingness is not manifestable outside the form of sensations. Consequently, to compare and contrast correctly there always remain only the contents of the forms of thinking and the forms of sensations. The form of thinking must be verified for the possibility and admissibility of the replacement (substitution) of its content by the verified objective content of the form of sensations. Then the form of thinking lays claim to the presence in it of the same objective content. Practice, embracing all the diversity of the thing activity of man, including the theoretical devices of the approbation of

knowledge, is the procedure of the verification of the possibility of the replacement (substitution) of the contents of the abstract forms by the contents of the concrete forms of any worth, and in particular and first of all by the content of the form of sensations. However, practice does not embrace such kinds of the verification and establishment of the contentfulness of the abstract forms as the substitution (replacement) of them by the forms also abstract, but of other levels, or by other, already verified abstractions with the confirmed truthfulness. Such a task is solved by the procedure of a more developed character, called interpretation. With the account of this circumstance we have at our disposal the complete set of the instruments or procedures of the establishment and verification of the contentfulness of the abstract constructions and structures of different kinds and levels (storiedness). As this becomes ever more evident, what is interesting is not the truthfulness of the abstract form in itself (which, it seems, is not the final aim of the human investigations and researches), but the ability of the content of this form to be expressed most completely by means of the contents of the more habitual for man forms of thingness, mediated by the forms of sensations, and the suitability of this content for the further "use". If such is observed, then the found abstract form, pushing apart for man the horizons of the perception and understanding of reality, is built into the general aggregate of the forms, replenishing thereby the contentful accumulations of this aggregate and contributing to the progressive cognition of reality, which "gropes" simultaneously by means of man and his thinking the most optimal contentful path of its own further development. The criterion of the truthfulness of the forms — their contentfulness. The truthfulness of the forms is assessed by the presence and "quantity" of this component. The status of the true acquires or obtains the form possessing the highest contentfulness of the possible and achievable at this definite stage of the development of reality. The assessment of the completeness, volume, or "quantity" of the contentfulness of this or that form is carried out by means of the procedure of interpretation (the expression or substitution, at times mental, of the content of the investigated form by the contents of the more concrete forms). The volume, quantity, reliability of the factual material, and indeed of the concrete forms in general, participating in the assessment procedure, and will determine in the final result the completeness of the contentfulness, and hence, also the truthfulness of the investigated form. Only those newly forming forms "are layered" and "are held" on the summit of the pyramid of the forms of reality which carry in themselves the content conjugating with the objective contents of the forms (constituting the foundation of the pyramid) and continuing by itself the chain of their objective contents. The pyramid of reality acquires stability if it is superstructed by the forms "stuffed" with objective content.

The replacement of the abstract forms by the more concrete forms (and more precisely, the replacement of the contents of the abstract forms by the contents of the more concrete forms) answers to the procedure of the filling of the abstract forms with senses or the endowing of them with objective content. By the return "to oneself at a new level" there is carried out the constant refinement of the content of the ever more abstracting summit form of the pyramid by way of the correlation of it with the objective content of the multilevel foundation of this same pyramid. The assessment of the contentfulness of the theoretical knowledge by practice is equivalent to the procedure of the representation of the content of the abstract forms by the replacing contents of the concrete thing forms, mediated by the forms of sensuousness and sensations (i.e., by the forms of empirical experience). The truthfulness or reliability of the abstract subjectively ideal forms (i.e., of knowledge: axioms, postulates, assertions, judgments, representations, theories, conceptions, teachings, paradigms, etc.), laying claim to the status of the carriers of objective content, is proved not by practice, but by the "quantity" or volume of the contentfulness of these generalizing forms. By the procedures of practice or interpretation there are established the presence and "quantity" of the contentfulness of the investigated forms. The presence of the contentfulness of the forms is determined by the possibility and admissibility of their interpretation by the factual material or concrete examples, and the "quantity" of the contentfulness is assessed by the number of such interpretations. The greater the number of the known concrete examples interpreting the investigated subjectively ideal abstract form, the with greater foundation this form can lay claim to the status of the carrier of objective content. In other words, the process of the search and establishment of truth is reduced to the procedure of the maximally complete expression of the uncognized irrational through the cognized rational (i.e., of the unknown through the known) and the return thereby to one's rational sources or foundations, but already at a new level — with the admixture of something special, as the supplemented rational. Thus, the criterion of truth is not practice in itself (the thing-practical and theoretical activity of the subject with its results), but the presence and completeness of the objective content in the investigated forms, which is established with the help of the procedure of practice or the technology of interpretation. Correctly generated and reproduced by thinking abstract forms turn out to be more contentful, since they include in themselves in the "removed" or

"folded" (i.e., compact, concentrated) form the data or contentful volumes of a whole multitude of concrete forms. The presence of a certain common in the particularities and concretions must be confirmed by the as large as possible number of such particularities and concretions. If this condition is fulfilled, then the "extracted" common acquires the status of objective content. After all, what in reality represents by itself this or that theory or conception or some foundation-laying principle which requires either the confirmation and acceptance, or the refutation? This is something common (which someone managed to "spy out" or notice accidentally or deliberately), correctly "extracted" from a certain number of particularities and concretions and concentratedly presented in the form of a generalized abstract theory (conception, model) for the explanation and comprehension of the essence of an even greater number of particularities and concretions (both existing and supposed to existence), as well as for the forecasting and anticipation of the emergence of the new forms of reality on the basis of the knowledge about this concentrated common. The truthfulness or reliability of this or that conception (theory, model) is proved by the presence in it of the objective content admitting the interpretation (i.e., the substitution, at least mental) of its content by the contents of the particular and concrete forms (examples, facts). If something abstractly common, constituting the essence of the conception (theory, model), is discovered in the particularities and concretions of the thing world and thereby admits the possibility of the interpretation of it by these particular and concrete forms of the thing world, then this abstractly common is viable and endowed with objective content, i.e., is true and reliable. Such a path of the establishment of truth fits into the single channel of the general contentful movement (from the "incomplete" truth to the ever more embracing and contentful truth), determined by the formulation of the principle of self-development. Thus are carried out both the process of cognition, and the processes of the development of anything whatsoever; another path simply does not exist! Proceeding from the fact that every just, true theory (conception, model) is interpreted or can be interpreted by the factual material, the admissibility and suitability of the newly advanced conception to the interpretation by the numerous facts serves as the unalterable condition requiring the verification for feasibility in the establishment of its reliability or truthfulness (or at least scientificity). In the channel of this approach this means that the possibility of the effective interpretation of any conception by an infinite number (or at least by a multitude) of facts endows this conception with the status of reliability (truthfulness); in other words, the greater the number of the correctly interpreting this or that theory facts is established, the more

reliable (truer) is the "invented" or generated by thinking theory (conception, model). As it has already been said earlier, every principle — this is something common, extracted from a whole row of particular separate examples and manifestations. The successful reverse "return" of the common into every new separate concrete particular (if such is possible, admissible, and achievable) and there is the way of the proof of the unity and commonness of the whole row of particularities and concretions, as well as the way of the proof of the presence of the objective content in all the investigated forms (i.e., in the compared particularities and concretions). The general principle (conception, theory) collects and unites by itself the separate particularities and concretions. By the gathering into one of the many particularities is determined the essence of the way of interpretation. If the interpretation (the gathering into one of the many particularities) succeeds, then the found or extracted earlier general principle can be recognized as objectively contentful and presented to the interpretation by an even greater number of particularities. The truthfulness of the principle is established if this principle is just and common in relation to some in advance given multitude of particularities and concretions. The greater the number of particularities and concretions is united by the commonness of the principle, the greater the foundation to consider it the truth (true). The more completely and large-scale the abstract is expressed by means of the concrete, the greater the number of concretions it embraces and unites — the more contentful it is, the more truly it is endowed with objective content. If the form growing by the abstract content does not include in itself the contents of the more concrete forms, is not expressed by them and by their aggregate in the most complete volume, is not substituted (at least mentally) by them, then it is not endowed with objective content and, consequently, is not true. The purpose of the technology of interpretation, in essence, and consists in the verification and proof of the presence of the objective content in all possible abstract forms. § 14. The differentiation of the forms by the volume of contentfulness. The volume of the contentfulness of a form as the indicator of the approximation to the expression of the objective content of reality In connection with the fact that the criterion of truth advanced here (that is, the contentfulness of the forms) turns out to be suitable for the quantitative assessment of the forms, it becomes possible to build the diverse forms by rank depending on the completeness or "quantity" of their contentfulness

(truthfulness). Such an approach opposes the myth of the possible equivalence of the different abstract forms (theories, conceptions, models, teachings, plans, etc.), including the identical truthfulness of the different points of view on one and the same question or subject of judgment. The true model, assessed as the most "close approximation" to the absolute truth at any given moment of development, — is always one; that is why it is important to be able to recognize such a model, in order to rely in one's further perspective movement-development precisely on it. As in any set (in any aggregate) of the forms of any level, exactly the same way at the level of the subjectively ideal forms there are encountered, in our opinion, both the less contentful forms, and the more contentful forms (the most "suitable" to the expression of the maximally achievable volume of content at the "summit" levels of the abstracted forms). Proceeding from such representations, the answer to the question — whether all the abstract constructions and structures, all the judgments (points of view, opinions, positions, decisions, conclusions, inferences, etc. in relation to any subject of judgment) are equal-righted, equal-powered, equal-sized, equal-valued, equivalent, and, finally, adequate, — is expectedly predictable and unambiguous: there is not and cannot be in principle equally adequate points of view on one and the same subject of judgment, since any and each of them represents by itself only the next approximation to the exhaustive expression of the objective content of the subject of judgment (i.e., only the approximation to a certain absolute truth). From all the judgments in such a case it is necessary, using for this the most complex criterion of the assessment of the truthfulness of the judgments, to choose the one whose contentful filling is maximal and most close to the expression of the objective content of reality (of the objective truth). All the other judgments represent by themselves even more coarse and inexact approximations. The task of the choice from two and more points of view of the most true or correct one at any moment of the choice will stand before man always, since the freedom of choice, proceeding from the indefiniteness and ambiguity of the course of events and the perspectives of development, with which the subject of cognition encounters and will always encounter, — is objective and conditioned by the dialectical nature of the world (of the developing reality). The choice from the row of the approximations of the most "close approximation" to the form of the highest contentfulness will always be the most responsible and dramatic moment in the destinies of mankind and of the separate individuals; and never will this decision be simple. One can only achieve the ever more meaningful striving to the increase of the contentfulness of such a decision. By virtue of the contentful-formal (dialectical) nature of the world and its development and of the resulting from here impossibility of the repetition or repeated reproduction of anything whatsoever in details and the smallest

particulars, — there is not and cannot be even two (not to mention a multitude) of equally true, identically equal-sized opinions, judgments, or points of view on one and the same subject (question, problem). One of the points of view (or judgments) will always be closer to the truth than the others. Precisely from this one must proceed, precisely this moment one must take into account in the determination of the truthfulness of this or that form and in the choice thereby of the landmark and foundation for the further investigations and development. When in application to this or that conception (theory) it is said wholesale that this is only one of the numerous equal-valued points of view or opinions in relation to this or that subject of cognition, one must, firstly, be informed about the principal unattainability of the equivalence or equal-valuedness of the different points of view on the subject of investigation and, secondly, remember about the necessity of the following of the certain rules or devices (procedures, technology) of the establishment of the truthfulness of the theoretical forms with the help of the complex criterion of their interpretability (by the verification by the factual material, concrete examples, reliable logical constructions and structures, etc.). Naturally, few people will believe only on the word in the fact that such-andsuch a conception is or can become an alternative knowledge in relation to the existing theories. Only by the presentation of the sufficient volume of the confirming factual material can one convince the interested public (or side) in the fact that this or that approach is at present the most "close approximation" to the true one. Proceeding from these considerations, one should expect that the special and heightened interest will always be presented by the assessment of the "contentfulnesses" of the above-mentioned forms by way of their comparison and, correspondingly, the determination of their priority by way of the ranking. Since each of the compared and ranked forms is in essence the next approximation to the form of the highest contentfulness, about the identity or equivalence of the compared and ranked forms there can be no speech. All the established, comprehended, and preparing to the comprehension truths — only the next approximations to the exhaustive expression of the content by the most perfect form, the next approximations to the absolute truth. But among these approximations there is always such which represents by itself the most close at any given moment to the "aim" approximation. Precisely it and must be able to find, precisely on it and must one orient oneself in one's investigations and in the adoption of the decisions. This is that present, that actual, that true which acquires the right to the long-term and stable existence, — on which and accentuates the attention G. W. F. Hegel, remarking: "Usually we call truth the agreement of the object with our representation... In the philosophical sense — on the contrary, truth in its abstract expression generally means the agreement of a certain content with itself... The untrue means... the bad, the non-

corresponding to itself... The examination of truth in the sense explained here, in the sense of the agreement with itself, constitutes the genuine task of logic" [3, p. 126—127]. To find such a form which would express the whole fullness of the content of reality at the given moment, i.e., to find the most contentful form, the form of the highest contentfulness, signifying by itself the identity of the components of the relation {content/form}, and, correspondingly, the degeneration of the latter into the indivisible wholeness — here is the genuine sense (from the position of our representations), enclosed in the cited words of Hegel. Truth is always expressed by the form most contentful at any given moment in comparison with all the other competing and accompanying forms. Supposing that the exhaustive expression of the content by the form is possible, admissible, and achievable only in god and through god: "Only God is the true correspondence of the notion and reality" [3, p. 126], — Hegel intuitively clothes the contentful movement of reality and its contentfulformal transformations into the attire of the iteration process of the infinite approximations to the expression of this very "truth in the last instance" (more laconically than Hegel in the light of his representations about this, perhaps, and one cannot say). In science (as, however, and in life) the objective (content) cannot be bypassed without consequences; in the quality of the "given from the age" there is the common, the foundation, the reproducible basis, on which something from the changing and subjective is layered, until this something acquires the status of the supplemented and developed objective. The particularities, peculiarities, and individualities all the same "dance from the stove", push off from the common, on which with time and are layered. The common and objective (content) takes place beyond any doubts, and all of us, willingly or unwillingly, strive to see and manifest to the world this objective content by means of the different forms. Everything that is "creative" subjective — this is only the approximations of different degrees (the multilevel models, fragments, or integral pictures), fixed in their striving to the "complete and final" expression and comprehension of the infinite contentful movement, i.e., of the development of this very common, foundation, objective. In the context of the considered material philosophy (as, however, and literature, and painting, and music, etc.) — this is the art of the subjective to see (discover) and manifest the objective (content). For the understandable reasons the successful one here turns out to be the one who manages to do this (such an approach easily and unerringly reveals the true subtext of the depth and greatness, say, of the Russian literature of the XIX century, etc.). The present approach has as its aim also to give the representation of that common, unifying objective which so far is not "grasped" by the modern

sciences (the latter, as in the parable with the elephant, each separately see either the legs, or the trunk, or the tail, not having the possibility to make up the representation about the whole). Without the single common objective foundation neither science, nor philosophy have the chances for the acceleration or the leap in their development. § 15. The "winding" of content as the essence of the principle of selfdevelopment What has been set forth in the preceding paragraphs fits into the channel of the concept of development, the main provisions of which and the description of the "mechanism" of the change-movement-development of reality are contained in the works [2, 8, 23]. At any given and arbitrary moment the world is unique and unrepeatable (which has already been noted above). But the unrepeatability or uniqueness of the changing and newly forming forms of reality is possible and realizable only thanks to the "winding", the "making of oneself from oneself", i.e., to the special specific character or way of the movement-development of the forms. This specific peculiarity of the development of reality must never fall out of the field of vision of the analysts engaged in theoretical investigations and the modeling of the changeability of the surrounding world. Under the "dictate" of the algorithm of the principle of self-development the correlation of the components-constituents in the known relation {change/basis} constantly changes, as a result of which at every moment there is realized the "changing basis", reproducing itself thanks to ever new and new contentful layers, — which is why it does not become exhausted, which is why the very development of reality does not cease. At every moment the "lived-through" becomes the "building material" for the "coming" present, "winding" and layering onto the still more distant past (the "lived-through"). Only such a way of movement-development as "the making of oneself from oneself", such a course of events, similar to "winding", insures and delivers the future from the detailed repetitions of the past. By the simple "copying" or "reflection" no single process is realized in full measure; the contrary — is from the category of delusions and chimeras. In reality there takes place the permanent movement in the direction of novelty and unrepeatability on the basis of the uniqueness of everything "accomplishing itself". The very character, type, and way of the movementself-development excludes the repeatability and reversibility of the forms. The appearance of new content in new forms cannot be explained in any way by the "copying" or "reflection" of the external world by the organs of sense (sensations) and by the thinking of the cognizing subject [11, p. 125, 151]. The new content arises and is realized in new forms because it "winds" one layer onto another with the accompanying changes of all the layers (by virtue of the absorption by the layers of these changes) and, correspondingly, with

the differences of the content of the subsequent forms from the content of the preceding ones. Man, animal, plant breathes, drinks, eats, interacts with the external world, and precisely in such a way — by the path of the layering of changes onto the basis — there is carried out the feeding, replenishment, renewal of the developing content of the living forms (including the subjectively ideal forms of human thinking). On the simplest things, on the ordinary and everyday examples one can be convinced of this again and again. In particular, the organism in the state of sleep is precisely that basis, that foundation onto which the changes and new volumes of content accumulated by the organism in the period of its activity are "mounted", into which they are built in. On the example of sleep it is well noticeable that the layers are attached to the basis not evenly and uninterruptedly, but by diverse portions, alternating with pauses, rest, "repose", etc. In what is seen the "sense" of the life or existence of the plant, animal, man? In that, by the different levels of their multilevel formations, to assimilate, express, comprehend the contentful accumulations in the calculation (with anticipation) of the subsequent assimilation, expression, and comprehension of new, still greater volumes of diverse contentful accumulations. Copying and photographing does not give true representations of the current processes in the multilevel reality (including at the level of self-cognition). As reflection or copying this can be perceived and carried out only in immobility, in statics, in the form of a particular case of the universal movement; in dynamics, in development — this is always "winding", "making from oneself"... The development-movement-change of actuality is the generation and embodiment of the objectively realizing principle of self-development (initially present in any "something", in any element of reality), which gives the representation and testifies about the intensifying movement of the content (of reality) onto the coverage and comprehension of the very same moving content in its ever more and more "embryonic" state.
